### [How to apply to AI safety fellowships (and beyond)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PiP4JqQFKhoqHGG2n/how-to-apply-to-ai-safety-fellowships-and-beyond)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">About a year ago, I decided to go all-in on applying to AI safety fellowships, and around the end of 2025 I got into MATS. I think there's a small art to communicating your skills legibly. When I've spoken with others about how I answer application questions, they seem to appreciate my advice. I wrote a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eFD3rozNCZKMe4rTs/mats-9-retrospective-and-advice" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MATS 9 Retrospective</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which was well-received, so consider this to be similar advice, but for applying to jobs or fellowships.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applying to AI safety fellowships or doing job applications is an adversarial process: The goal of an application process is to measure how well the candidate would do in the position they're applying for, but this process is noisy. Some candidates will (inevitably) try to overfit to the application process itself, in a way that oversells their abilities.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a grey area between "how to make your extant talents legible and understandable" and "how to fool people into seeing talents that aren't there". I've tried hard to withhold advice which could be used to overfit to the applications process, and to focus on advice that differentially helps people who are fit for the job but struggle to communicate this to the reviewer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many application processes have significant flaws that lead to them being noisier than they should be (including those at companies you think should know better). I'm unsure why this is the case, I suspect the issue is that this process is recreated at ~every company, and every company thinks they're a special snowflake with special hiring requirements such as "very smart people". Put less cynically: hiring is a hard problem, people who get good at hiring often get promoted away from hiring, and there are often very few ways for feedback to flow from the applicants to the people doing the hiring.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With my disclaimers out of the way, I'll split the rest of this into some advice for making your skills legible in general and then some advice specific to AI safety fellowships.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to apply</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Differentiate yourself from the Average Joe</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There will be lots of Average Joes applying alongside you, and you want to make sure you're not mistaken for an Average Joe. Put yourself in the mind of the reviewer: the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vast</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> majority of applications will be from Average Joe. The application before yours, and the application after yours, will be from Average Joe. Average Joe is great, very fun to talk to. But Average Joe is not quite cut out for the job. You've done cool and interesting things, Average Joe has done mediocre and mildly interesting things. We love Average Joe, but probably wouldn't hire them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the application process and as you're describing your experience, your projects and accomplishments, it is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">absolutely critical</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that whatever you write could not be mistaken for something Average Joe could write.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let me be crystal clear: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it is insufficient to accurately describe the impressive things you've done.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You must describe it in a way that maximally distances yourself from what an Average Joe could have written.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are hundreds of Average Joes applying to this position, and only one of you. There is substantial overlap between the best-written applications from Average Joes and the worst-written application from yourself. You're going up against the best-written application from all Average Joes from across the world, so you need to put a lot of effort into writing things that no Average Joe could write. Every sentence should be evidence that you are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> an Average Joe, so that you maximally distinguish yourself from the rest of the applicant pool. I do quite literally mean you should read each sentence you write during an application, and ask "If I didn't know the person who wrote this, what's my </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lowest possible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> estimate for their skills and abilities?". You need to ensure the lowest estimate is as high as possible.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Making your skills legible</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The book </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Seeing Like A State</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> gives a slightly new meaning to the word </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">legible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that's extremely useful. You can have the skills needed to do a job, but this is basically irrelevant when considering whether you'll get the position or not. The only thing that matters is whether you can convince the person reviewing your application that you have these skills. If you cannot </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">legibly communicate</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the reviewer that you have the skills needed for the job, they will reject your application.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is critical: it is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">insufficient</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be cracked. You </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">must</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be able to communicate to someone and have them understand that you are cracked. If you cannot communicate your skills, you might as well not have them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hard</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to properly communicate your skills via an online application. This is partly because writing is hard, but it's also partly because there are so many other people who don't have your skills but are trying to claim that they do. It's very hard to separate honesty from deception if all you have are some interview questions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is why many people get jobs via recommendations or friends-of-friends: Recommendations are generally terrible at communicating the skill of the person, but the recommender is inherently staking their social capital on saying "this person is honest and won't try to trick you when you evaluate whether they're fit for the job".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In lieu of getting a recommendation from someone, you will need to convince whoever's reviewing your application that 1. you're fit for the job and 2. you're better than the other people who are also applying. This is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">different</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from just being fit for the job and being better than the other people who are applying: I'll assume you actually are fit for the job, so the challenge is in effectively (and honestly!) communicating this to the reviewer.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concrete ways to be more legible</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The requirement for skills to be legible is why certificates, PhDs, degrees, references, and journal publications are often requested as evidence: they're (usually) hard to fake and communicate your skills in a way that's standardised and easy to understand.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contributions to open source projects are okay, but significantly less legible because they require that the reviewer understands the project and understands your niche technical contributions to that project. With modern AI coding, open-source contributions say less about your programming ability than they did in the past.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Put yourself in the reviewer's shoes</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a bit tricky to express properly, I fear I'll explain </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> idea, but not the correct idea and you'll come away thinking you know what I'm gesturing at but nonetheless I failed to explain the idea properly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you answer a question, it's helpful to ask yourself: what's the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">least</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> qualified Average Joe who could reasonably write that answer? You want to ensure the least qualified Average Joe who could reasonably write your answer is still someone who'd get accepted.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Less adversarially, you should try to imagine the state of the reviewer's mind as they read your answer</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and try to make them more likely to accept you. Often applications will ask something like:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What's the most impressive thing you've built or accomplished? (50 words)</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And it's important to realise that you're answering the question behind the question. Your goal is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to describe the most impressive thing you've built, your goal is to describe the most impressive thing you've built that can be explained in 50 words and is likely to get you accepted. Often (but not always!) these will be the same. If the most impressive thing you've built requires 40 words of background just to explain the context, but the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">second</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> most impressive thing you've built is easy and quick to explain, you should describe the second most impressive thing.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The internet is more meritocratic, so use it</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The internet (mostly) doesn't care where you're living or who you know. If you're disadvantaged because you don't live in the right area or don't know the right people or didn't go to the right school, you should put more effort into your online presence than you otherwise would. I don't think most people appreciate that ~everyone is online. It might be really tricky to get important people to respond to your email, but if you write something interesting that shows up in their feed, they're quite likely to read it!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You should write publicly if you are talented but lack the credentials which usually make those talents legible. Well-written and informative technical essays tend to go viral on the websites that matter: technical people </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">love</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to read deep-dives into peculiar topics. If you have skills and a good understanding of niche areas, you can make these skills legible by writing things online (in English) and sharing them on LessWrong/HackerNews. Having a personal website is useful but not critical.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is especially the case if you're not in the Bay Area or otherwise in a scene where you can discuss your thoughts in person. Writing online is a way to share your thoughts with people who'd otherwise never give you the time of day.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This extends (somewhat) to Twitter. Having a stronger presence online is a very high leverage way to get the attention of very powerful people</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Everyone uses the same internet, and everyone scrolls roughly the same timeline. It's significantly easier to get your ideas in front of interesting people via Twitter than (for example) by flying to San Francisco and knocking on the front door of the organisation you want to work for. Powerful people are usually open to hearing interesting ideas, but they can't allow any random person to book a 20 minute meeting to discuss their interesting idea. The internet is an incredibly powerful way of getting good ideas in front of people who matter, and if you do this well you can often use this credibility to unlock other opportunities.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While it's commonly done, I'd recommend against putting your efforts into building the following of an anonymous Twitter account. It's very hard to "cash out" that reputation into opportunities or job offers without removing the anonymity. While putting your name and face to your opinions is scary, people (in my experience) are more likely to engage with a named profile precisely </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">because</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> there's a human who's staking some small amount of reputation on this opinion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That being said, it is hard to build a following on Twitter if you don't have a few friends such that you can all mutually like each other's tweets (and so launder each other's credibility). Laundering credibility is incredibly common practice (both online and offline) so it's useful to find friends who can vouch for you in this way. Twitter does do weird things with country borders and limiting the reach of posts based on where you're posting from, but I still think the above advice is applicable. For example, I saw a significant increase in my following and reach when I was posting from London or The Bay compared to when I was posting from South Africa (although this is confounded by MATS and gaining followers from friends at MATS, so possibly this is less of an effect than I'm making it out to be).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ensure that skimming your CV still leaves a good impression</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I look at your CV for 15 seconds, what information do I see? I should see things that are directly relevant to the application, for example language model and research experience. There's a lot written about CVs and how to make a good one, and I imagine your favourite LLM could help you with the layout and styling, but don't trust it to write the prose for you. You should try to view your CV with fresh eyes and notice what jumps out at you during the first 15 seconds. If someone had to make a decision knowing nothing else, would they accept or reject you?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I look at it from 5 metres away, what stands out? I sure hope it's things like "Intern @ Impressive Company" or "Built Cool Project" and not "The internship was from January to March 2025". If someone only reads the headings on your CV, do they come away wanting to give you the position or not? Do the headings differentiate you from the Average Joe, or could Average Joe have written exactly the same headings as you have?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CVs tend to have lots of structured data (e.g. 3 items of job experience, where each job has a start date, end date, company name, location, etc). This can cause them to become bloated with redundant information that's implied by other parts of your CV. You should look carefully at every word on your CV, and ask if it's pulling its weight. I do mean literally every word. If you studied at the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">University of &lt;City&gt;</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, you probably don't need to also include that you were studying in the location of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&lt;City&gt;, &lt;Country&gt;</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I say you don't need to include it, because (in general) the location won't make someone more or less likely to hire you. You do literally need to look at each word and ask if removing the word would make someone less likely to hire you. Every word should make a reviewer more eager to get you on their team ASAP.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider how you can improve yourself</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notice the little seed of doubt that appears in your mind when an application implicitly asks for something you don't have. Google Scholar? PhD? Which Ivy League? Not all of these are solvable, but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some of them are</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and you should listen to what the applications are telling you they want. If you see lots of applications asking if you've done ARENA, then </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you should try to do ARENA</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If you see lots of applications asking "Share things you've done related to AI Safety" then you should try to do lots of things in AI safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Basically, you should look at each application as both a potential for you to get in, but also a very strong signal for what you need to do next time round to better your chances. If you're applying to the fellowships over and over again and answering the questions in basically the same way each time, you'll probably continue to get rejected. Take the questions as a rubric against which you should improve yourself.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A little love letter for </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">typst</span></code></h2><p><a href="https://typst.app/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Typst</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is great, it's like LaTeX but significantly easier to use and faster. I spent some time migrating my CV to Typst and it's paid off massively. I can strongly recommend it, especially nowadays when doing this is just one prompt away.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part of the benefit is that it's programmable, so my CV looks something like:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">= Boyd Kane CV</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">== Experience</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#from-yaml("snippets/2026-mats-extension.yaml")</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#from-yaml("snippets/2026-mats.yaml")</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#from-yaml("snippets/2024-aisct.yaml")</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And each of those </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#from-yaml(...)</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> functions reads a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/essays/yaml"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">YAML</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> file that looks something like:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><pre class="code-block"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">title: MATS 9 with Alex Turner &amp; Alex Cloud</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">location: Berkeley, California</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">from: "Jan 2026"</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">until: "Apr 2026"</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">desc: |</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  - The project by my coauthor </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#link</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">("https://joneedssleep.github.io/")[Jo Jiao] &amp; I was accepted as one of 9 MATS </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">#link</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">("https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4nsCTYRS1H4")[Symposium Spotlight talks] out of a cohort of ~100 fellows, and will be continued during the MATS 9 Extension in London. It has been accepted for publication in NeurIPS 2026 (reviewer scores: 5/5/4 out of 6).</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  - LLMs behave differently in evaluations than they do when we're not watching them. It's possible that an LLM would behave misaligned in some very narrow range of scenarios (e.g. when it could exfiltrate its weights _and_ nobody's watching _and_ it has internet access _and_ ...).</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  - Alex Turner (ex-Google DeepMind) and Alex Cloud (Anthropic) are my mentors as I work on a project to distinguish between LLMs that would exhibit this behaviour and LLMs that won't.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  - This project uses finetuning as a method of evaluation: by measuring how easily an untrusted LLM is able to learn some misaligned behaviour, we can get an estimate for how likely the unfinetuned LLM is to behave misaligned.</span><br /></pre></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then when I compile the </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.typ</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> doc, it gives me a CV listing that looks like this:</span></p><img alt="mats-cv.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790977586/lexical_client_uploads/isfmgfqtetgntidokyrw.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">great</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I can easily add/remove items from my CV without messing with the formatting. And as a side-effect, I've got an LLM-friendly log of every project, job, internship, talk, or thing of interest I've ever done. Which brings me to:</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maintain an LLM-friendly version of your work experience</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few years ago I converted all the job-relevant projects and positions I've done into YAML files (as described above). At the time this was just so I could move things over to Typst, but this has been doubly useful in that it allows me to easily describe what I've done to LLMs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are many ways in which this is valuable, but to highlight one which was critical to me applying to many AI safety fellowships while working full-time. It was pretty tough to get motivation some nights to actually go through </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">yet another</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> application process (especially in the beginning). Nearly every question gave me serious imposter syndrome and I'd often be left with writer's block trying to find an answer, questioning why I should even bother. Most questions are phrased in a way that made me doubt whether anything I had done was enough:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are 1–2 reasons why you would be a good fit to do empirical AI research? Please give a concise description of each piece of evidence and explain why it’s relevant. Max 200 words</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3">[3]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How experienced are you with tuning hyperparameters: Write about your hyperparameter tuning experiences. For LLMs, what tricks do usually try to make them work at a target task?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3">[3]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you consider to be your top achievement(s)? (Max. 50 words) Please share 1-3 achievements that you are especially proud of. These could be scholastic, like your academic performance at university or in an international olympiad, and extracurricular, like winning a competitive sporting event.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3">[3]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In these scenarios, I'd use LLMs to remind me of the interesting things I've done that are relevant here. This </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very consistently</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reminded me of extremely relevant experience that I have and projects that I've done. The prompt was something like:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's my experience and a question for an AI safety fellowship. Do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> answer the question for me, but give a bullet list of relevant parts of my background that I should mention in my response.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Making it easy for myself to answer many questions like the above was very important for me being able to do well in the application process.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll repeat this again: Do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NOT</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> get LLMs to respond to the questions for you. I gave Opus 5.5 the above question along with my pre-MATS context, and the response it gave failed to make a good case for why I should be accepted. Opus does </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very well</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at answering the question as it is posed: it cites 2 projects I had done. However, Opus fails to answer the meta-question that sits behind </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> application questions: "Why should I accept this application?".</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keep a text log of your questions/responses</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you end up applying to many positions, you're going to answer many nearly-identical questions. You should keep a log (mine was just a </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">faq.md</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> file) of each question you got asked and what you answered. This is immensely helpful:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you get through to the next interview round, many interviewers will refer to your answers and you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> don't want to be struggling to remember what you said.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes, different applications will ask ~identical questions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-4">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about your background, why you want to work in AI safety, etc. Being able to refer to a previous response and lightly edit it will help you fill out more applications without becoming more tired.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reuse your answers if they're irrelevant to the question. The reason you're applying to one application should </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be the same as the reason you're applying to another application. If you think they're the same, you need to go dig deeper and figure out why they're different.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If in doubt, you should probably default to writing the answer </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">de novo</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Even for something generic-sounding like "Describe your current career plans and aspirations", you should phrase your career plans in a way that emphasises your goals as relevant to the position you're currently applying for.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> make up fake career plans just to match with the job. That way leads to sadness.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">should</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> prioritise the aspects of your existing career plans that the mentor would be most interested in seeing. Do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> bend the truth about what you want to do. But if your career plan involves something that's mostly irrelevant to the current position, don't go yapping about it for ages and ages.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applying to MATS and other AI-safety fellowships</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These sections are primarily phrased with technical AI safety fellowships in mind, although I think there's lots of advice that is relevant to other job applications.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Safety fellowship pipeline</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like it or not, there's a bit of a pipeline to the AI safety fellowships, and some are easier to get into if you've done an earlier-stage fellowship first. Getting into a later-stage fellowship without any prior qualifications is hard. Doing earlier stage fellowships is a really good way to make your skills legible, because you get more time and input from progressively more important people who can later recommend you for progressively more interesting and impactful fellowships.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm going to be a bit explicit about this pipeline (and risk offending some people who disagree with me) because even if I get the details wrong, I think it's valuable to communicate that there </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a pipeline, and applying to a fellowship without enough legible skills will possibly result in you feeling despondent and low-value when you're rejected. Skipping the earlier stages of the pipeline </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> possible and I encourage people to try. But I think it's important to communicate that your odds of getting into the Anthropic Fellows Program are not the same as your odds of getting into SPAR. Here's Opus 5.5's rough ranking, and I approximately agree: </span><a href="https://claude.ai/share/9ecdbf1f-604c-426b-a4ca-da58545ac8cd"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://claude.ai/share/9ecdbf1f-604c-426b-a4ca-da58545ac8cd</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you feel bitter because you've been rejected and you believe the fellowships only accept Ivy League graduates, my advice is to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">make your skills more legible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Being an Ivy League graduate is a very legible indicator of skill, but so are options such as earlier-stage AI safety fellowships or getting involved with (or starting!) your local AI safety interest group.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different streams are more or less competitive</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can't speak for every fellowship, but generally you don't apply to just The Fellowship, you actually apply to multiple streams within The Fellowship. This is very consequential! When submitting your application, you're not competing with everyone who applied to The Fellowship, you're just competing with the people who applied to the stream. So while MATS is extremely competitive, your preferred stream within MATS might not be as competitive. This is especially the case if you're interested in relatively niche ideas for that fellowship that probably get fewer applicants. If you see a stream that you think fits your interests very well but you think you're underqualified for the fellowship, consider applying anyway.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply to multiple streams and to multiple fellowships</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fellowship applications are noisy, so if your skills are not maximally legible you'll probably be rejected even though you shouldn't have. Similarly, someone else will likely be accepted even though they should have been rejected. There's a fair amount of luck involved, and applying to more fellowships gives you more chances at being lucky.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Luckily, there are several fellowships, and each of them has several streams. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do not spam the applications process with pointless applications</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But please </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> apply to more than one stream or fellowship if multiple options seem to be a good fit. Making your skills more legible increases your chances of being accepted into any single fellowship or stream, but applying to multiple fellowships/streams gives you more chances to get lucky.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's a bit tricky to give advice on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how many</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> streams you should apply to. Doing the application is usually a lot of work, and doing it well is more on top of that. I can maybe give advice on when to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> apply to a stream: if you're answering the questions or doing the take-home assessment and you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">just can't be bothered</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, you notice yourself struggling to find the motivation, or there are just many other things that seem interesting in that moment, you should consider not finishing the application. Most streams or fellowships will have questions on the application that are somewhat similar to the kind of work you'll do during the stream/fellowship, so if you're struggling for motivation to answer the questions, you'll likely struggle for motivation during the fellowship itself.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The mentors (often) make the final decision</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As said above, for some fellowships, you actually apply to a particular stream and then the mentor(s) of that stream make the acceptance decision. If possible, you should heavily tailor your application to your mentor's interests. Don't be fake or deceptive! But if (for example) you did a small project looking at the security implications of hardware side-channels but you normally exclude this from your applications because it's not usually relevant to AI safety, but the mentor you're applying to happened to also be interested in side-channels as they relate to ASI, then you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">absolutely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> should customise your CV and application to describe this project on hardware side-channels.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like all things, this is about making your skills legible to the person reviewing your application. Most people don't understand hardware side-channels so there's no point in including the project in every application you make. But if a reviewer has prior experience in side-channels, they're more likely to see the skills required for your particular project, so describing your side-project is a very legible way of showing them how good you are.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In general, if you've got experience in some niche field and your potential mentor happens to have previous work in that field, you should consider highlighting this in your application. With this in mind, you should try to read as much as you can (with your eyeballs, not Claude's!) about the person who'll be reviewing your application and making the final decision. This includes their blog, online profiles and research papers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don't make the mistake of faking alignment with your mentor's values. At best this will get you into the fellowship you want but then you've got to spend three months doing something you dread. There are better things to do than waste your own time pursuing a goal you don't care about. Gaining prestige in a field you don't care about is like climbing the wrong mountain and being surprised that you didn't get the view you wanted.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're a "risky" candidate, try start with low-commitment fellowships</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think you're skilled but can't seem to get anyone's attention, it might be that you're too high-risk at the moment. Mentors might be looking at your application and thinking "this person will </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">either</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be great or be atrocious, I can't risk 3 months of my time on that". This is a rational decision on their part, and you can change their mind by becoming less risky. It can be hard to know if you're a risky option, it requires putting yourself into a reviewer's shoes and looking through your application critically. Becoming more legible can often make you less risky, for example doing another less-prestigious fellowship or working on a related personal project.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how do you get into a fellowship if this requires having already gotten into a fellowship‽ The answer is that you start with the low-stakes fellowships (online, shorter, no funding) and progress towards higher and higher stakes fellowships (in-person, longer, more funding).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It can be tricky to take a lower-stakes option because it implies that you were less capable than you thought you were. You might have the skills to do great things, but if you cannot convince other people that you have these skills then you will not be given the opportunity to do these great things. The good thing is that there's always a lower-stakes option that you can do to make your skills more legible, regardless of what you get rejected from. I strongly recommend </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">against</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> applying to the same position over and over again unless you've significantly improved yourself and made your skills more legible in between the different applications.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lower stakes options might look like doing some or all of the ARENA course in your free time on Google Colab GPUs, or publicly writing up your thoughts on a paper you read (bonus points if you disagree with the author's decisions). I recommend ARENA because it is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> highly regarded in the AI safety community but there's no application process in between you today and the you who's completed the syllabus. Average Joe has not done ARENA by themselves. I recommend writing your thoughts because </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">many</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI safety research questions ask you to read a particular paper and then either suggest follow-up experiments or to critique the paper. If you've got public critique of papers or have run follow-up experiments on other experiments you thought might be interesting, this is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incredibly valuable</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to people trying to assess whether you're a good fit. Average Joe has not bothered to write substantive feedback on popular research papers.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider an 80,000 Hours advising call</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I applied and got a call, and found this very helpful in getting a third party to highlight my weaknesses and confirm my strengths. The advisor also offered various follow-ups with different industry professionals who would have been extremely useful, had I not been accepted into MATS shortly afterwards.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fellowships are ~constantly accepting new applications</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While they don't literally have rolling applications (yet) the frequency for the big fellowships is much more than annual. If you want to apply, there's no need to wait until the applications actually open for you to prepare your CV. You should start </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">now</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to get your CV and thoughts in order, instead of waiting for the applications to open and then having to grind to get things ready in time. You can pre-emptively look at mentors from previous cohorts (they'll likely mentor again) to see who you want to apply to. You can also look at research papers from mentors you admire to see what sort of work they do and whether you think you'd enjoy doing that same work.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your success is roughly proportional to your effort per application</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can definitely always put more effort into </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">manually</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (not with an LLM) looking through everything about an application. You should </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">heavily</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> study the mentors on the streams you're excited about, and read their recent papers. If you can be critical while doing so ("why did they choose X hyperparameter?", "why didn't you consider Y?") this is even better, most mentors appreciate thoughtful critique and discussion of their work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is definitely a rabbit hole for some mentors, there's always more you can do and more you can learn about them. I'm not sure when is the right place to stop, but you should be trying to discover if the work they do is something you'd like to do yourself. If you're not excited by their research, you probably don't want to apply to work on their stream, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">even if they're "famous"</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Look through ~all the mentors' pages</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a bit of a nightmare. For the bigger streams there are often dozens of mentors, and there are often a half-dozen fellowships. I'm not going to pretend it isn't a lot of work, but it is hard to avoid if you want to be selective with your time. I relied on heavily filtering early on (e.g. I had to completely ignore governance and non-US options in order to make the workload manageable) and from there I just spent a lot of time reading their biographies and websites.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Luckily, this is a process you can start in advance, before applications for that fellowship technically open. Even if the "official" mentor pages haven't opened up yet, many fellowships will have a list of their previous mentors (e.g. </span><a href="https://www.matsprogram.org/mentors"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://www.matsprogram.org/mentors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) and probably you can get Claude to surface useful information like previous papers and research interests.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you get through to a later interview stage, it's probably worth taking a day off of work</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I forget when, but at some point I had an enormous number of stream applications due in just a few days' time window. I was working full time, and most of the applications assume you're able to answer their questions 24/7. I ended up taking a sick day off of work and just read &amp; wrote applications the whole day. Looking back, I think this was well worth my time to do, although I'm not sure if I'd have felt differently if I hadn't gotten into MATS. Because I was fresh and well-rested, I managed to get a lot more done than I would otherwise and that particular day at work wasn't very special or unique.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rejection feels like shit</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's really not fun. It hurts, and makes you question whether you should bother doing anything to get better. To the extent that you can, I don't think you should beat yourself up about getting a rejection letter. But it is hard, and many rejection letters do very little to cushion the blow or suggest productive next steps.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After taking a break and clearing your head, I recommend trying to review all the answers you gave to all the questions. Review your CV, your references, and your online presence. Ask how you can change these things to be closer to a promising AI safety researcher. Often there's a several-month gap between one round of AI safety applications and the next, so I encourage you to be ambitious with what you can do in those several months.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The goal here is to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">learn</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the application process, and to try to avoid feeling dejected. You have incredibly valuable information now about what does work and what doesn't. You should do your best to learn from this information such that you can spend the next few months doing ambitious projects that improve your chances for the next cycle. Try to get involved with a local AI safety org (or think about starting one). Make your work more legible.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You should probably self-study the ARENA curriculum</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I knew ARENA was good, but if you've got some time to work through the content on the ARENA schedule, it's seen as very high signal and many applications explicitly ask if you've done ARENA. They didn't seem to strongly discriminate between doing it on your own online or in-person. If I hadn't gotten into MATS, working through the ARENA coursework on my own would have been my top priority.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A long list of application questions</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are all the application questions I answered, slightly deduplicated, anonymised, and sorted arbitrarily. If you're wanting to apply for an AI safety fellowship but there are none available, I strongly recommend going through this list and either answering each question, or considering what you can do to make your future answer to each question the best it possibly can be.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Show all the questions</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are there any previous projects or experiences you’d like to highlight as especially relevant? For e.g. you can highlight if you've previously participated in fellowships such as PIBBSS, MATS, ERA, Pivotal, Talos, or similar programs and include the dates in which you did so.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are you interested in working in a team of four talented, conscientious people (plus us as mentors)? We think people who answer “yes” to this question are probably a better fit for our stream, but it is not a strict requirement.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could you give us a sense of what your longer-term career paths might be? This will help set the agenda for our call, so think of at least two different career paths you could see yourself pursuing and list some pros and cons for each.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Describe a time when you faced a non-technical challenge collaborating with other people on a complex project. How did you address it? (Suggested length: ~250 words) Feel free to share other information that would help us understand what it's like to be your teammate.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Describe a time where you had to demonstrate high agency as described </span><a href="https://www.neelnanda.io/blog/44-agency"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? (100 - 200 words) This refers to a time where you took initiative to solve a problem, create something new, or drive a project forward despite obstacles or uncertainty. This could be academic, professional, or personal.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Describe your current career plans and aspirations</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Explain your strongest disagreement with other alignment thinkers. Consider only your own inside-view understanding, and don't defer to others' expertise. (Suggested length ~250 words, but it's okay to write more)</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For [Multi-Agent safety, AI for Facilitating Human Cooperation, Gradual Disempowerment], describe the research you'd like to undertake during the fellowship and why you think it is important?</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Give an example of an empirical AI safety research project you’d be interested in working on, and why? Please mention any relevant experience if applicable. Max 300 words</span></li><li value="10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have you completed Chapter 0 of the ARENA curriculum, or, if you join our stream, would you commit to completing it before the start of the program (Jan 5)?</span></li><li value="11"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have you done surveys/literature reviews? Or show some evidence on how fast you can read papers, and what kind of useful takeaways you usually obtain from papers. Put "N/A" if this question is not applicable.</span></li><li value="12"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How experienced are you with tuning hyperparameters: Write about your hyperparameter tuning experiences. For LLMs, what tricks do usually try to make them work at a target task?</span></li><li value="13"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How long do you configure the deep learning environment: Describe one of your past experience for the most complicated server that you’ve set up the deep learning environment on. What did you do, and how long does the entire configuration take? (Basically configuration here means everything before you can start to run the program.)</span></li><li value="14"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How will [Fellowship] help you to achieve your long-term career aspirations? What are you hoping to get out of the program? (max. 800 characters)</span></li><li value="15"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re open to working in a team, briefly reflect on how you might make this go well. What would a great collaboration look like to you? In your answer, you might draw on prior experience in teams, but you don’t have to. (max 250 words).</span></li><li value="16"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine it's 2027 and you completed [Fellowship] a year ago. What specific role or project are you now working on that represents your highest-impact contribution to AI safety or governance? (100-200 words)</span></li><li value="17"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 1-2 sentences per project, please provide a short summary of the project and what you contributed to it.</span></li><li value="18"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is there anything in particular you're looking to get out of our potential collaboration? It's okay to leave this blank. In that case, we'll assume your answer is "I want to grow as an ML researcher and make contributions to AI safety."</span></li><li value="19"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Key factors of AI progress in the next ~5 years? (max. 1500 characters)</span></li><li value="20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Link 5 papers or blogposts relevant to AI control, oversight or model evaluations that you're excited about (they don’t have to be your own). We may ask you about these in interviews.</span></li><li value="21"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Link to a personal blog post</span></li><li value="22"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Link to code samples from past projects (e.g. GitHub links or uploaded zip files), ideally a substantial project and (if possible) a machine learning project (these could be the same or different projects).</span></li><li value="23"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Links to things you've done or your experience</span></li><li value="24"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mitigating Gradual Disempowerment. Develop concepts and tools that will allow us to develop mitigations to preserve human agency and ensure that our institutions serve us. (500 - 1000 words, 3 hour proctored time limit)</span></li><li value="25"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other notable skills or experience</span></li><li value="26"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please add a link to 1–2 writing samples, code repositories, or similar that are illustrative of your knowledge or skills relevant to the fellowship, and then briefly give any important context on this piece of work (e.g. whether other people helped produce it).</span></li><li value="27"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please describe a hard technical or research problem you solved. What was the bottleneck and how did you overcome it? What impact did solving the problem have? (200 words)</span></li><li value="28"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please link impressive AI projects that you've built.</span></li><li value="29"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please provide 1-2 code samples from past projects (e.g. GitHub links or files).</span></li><li value="30"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please provide links to any of your projects or posts related to AI safety.</span></li><li value="31"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please share any academic, professional, or personal accomplishments you are proud of. (max 200 words)</span></li><li value="32"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please share any other information about your background in ML that you would like us to know.</span></li><li value="33"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please talk briefly about an area of technical work right now you’re most interested in or excited about, and why. (~3 sentences)</span></li><li value="34"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Propose a follow-up experiment to section 3 of this </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.06278"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and explain the relevance of the experiment to AI safety efforts. (Suggested length: ~250 words for experiment, 1-4 sentences for relevance.)</span></li><li value="35"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Provide two reference contacts. These contacts should ideally relate to your past AI, AI safety, research, engineering, and/or other relevant experience.</span></li><li value="36"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Question: What do you consider to be your top achievement(s)? (Max. 50 words) Please share 1-3 achievements that you are especially proud of. These could be scholastic, like your academic performance at university or in an international olympiad, and extracurricular, like winning a competitive sporting event.</span></li><li value="37"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Read the following report by Model Evaluation &amp; Threat Research (METR): Measuring AI Ability to Complete Long Tasks. For additional technical details, you can see the full-length paper here. In section 7.2.1 of the full paper, the authors discuss "systematic differences between our tasks and real tasks", including automatic scoring, no interaction with other agents, lax resource constraints, unpunishing, and static environments. Which one of these systematic differences do you expect to have the largest impact on the generalizability of the study's results? Explain your reasoning. (1600 characters)</span></li><li value="38"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Take a look at the research projects for [Mentor]'s stream that I'm interested in (Reasoning Model Interpretability, Red-Teaming and Elicitation, and Model Organisms). What are 1-3 pieces of evidence that you’d be able to do good research in this stream? They don't have to be standard credentials! Please describe them and why they're relevant. Aim for conciseness (e.g. a single page is likely sufficient). Feel free to write up a high level plan for a research project you'd want to work on as evidence that you are able to generate and/or flesh out interesting directions that we could work on together.</span></li><li value="39"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tell us about a recent achievement where you displayed proactivity, grit, and self-motivation</span></li><li value="40"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tell us about any interest or involvement you've had in effective altruism.</span></li><li value="41"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tell us about your most significant technical accomplishment.</span></li><li value="42"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What (if any) relevant experience do you have for working in the track you have selected? (750 characters)</span></li><li value="43"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What NLP/general AI tasks do you usually read papers about, e.g., AI safety, LLM interpretability, LLM for coding, computational social science, etc. (and how many papers for each task, as a rough estimation)?</span></li><li value="44"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are 1–2 reasons why you would be a good fit to do empirical AI research? Please give a concise description of each piece of evidence and explain why it’s relevant. Max 200 words</span></li><li value="45"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are some (possible) long-term career goals for you (e.g., a bit like "where do you see yourself in your 40s")?</span></li><li value="46"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are your career aspirations? (100 - 250 words) Please outline 2-3 potential career paths you're considering and explain how this fellowship fits into your trajectory.</span></li><li value="47"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are your long-term career aspirations? (max. 800 characters) We ask this to better understand your interests and motivations. We know that career plans often evolve and might be broad at this point, so feel free to express uncertainty.</span></li><li value="48"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are your long-term career goals</span></li><li value="49"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What attracts you to [Company]'s mission of protecting humanity from AI risks?</span></li><li value="50"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What concerns you the most about AI development?</span></li><li value="51"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What deep learning models have you coded before (e.g., adaptations of open-weight LLMs, VLMs, etc)? If applicable, support your answer with your GitHub repos</span></li><li value="52"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you hope to gain from participating in [Fellowship]? (200 words)</span></li><li value="53"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you think is the most promising way to gain helpful information about the probability that advanced AI's are scheming right now? (&lt;300 words)</span></li><li value="54"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you want to work on? Please give a 3-5 paragraph pitch for your research idea that fits this stream.</span></li><li value="55"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What have been your preferred sources for learning about AI Safety topics? Please be specific (e.g., name particular researchers, papers, podcasts, courses, books rather than general categories).</span></li><li value="56"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is a result in AI Safety, Governance, and/or ML that you have found interesting/surprised you? Did it make you change your mind about something? (max. 1000 characters)</span></li><li value="57"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is an important opinion or idea about AI that you wish more people understood? Please explain the opinion or idea in 200 words or fewer. Separately, in 150 words or less, explain why you wish more people understood it and why that might be important.</span></li><li value="58"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is one potential project or research question you would be interested to work on as part of [Fellowship]? (2000 characters) You don’t need a fully scoped research idea at this stage, and nothing you write here is a commitment. If you're accepted to the programme, your project can evolve substantially (and may differ entirely) after you meet your research manager and mentors. However, it is a useful exercise to brainstorm what you could actually work on, and it helps us understand your research interests. Please make an effort to highlight key sub-questions and the theory of change (how would the research ultimately lead to reducing large-scale catastrophic risks from advanced AI?).</span></li><li value="59"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is your aim for this research collaboration/internship? Answer using bullet points the following:</span></li><li value="60"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is your favourite piece of research/theory? Why?</span></li><li value="61"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is your motivation for applying to [the Empirical Research] workstream? Max 150 words</span></li><li value="62"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is your motivation to apply to this programme? What do you hope to get out of it?</span></li><li value="63"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What knowledge areas are you familiar with, or do you like to spend your time thinking about? If applicable, share a link of your notes etc.</span></li><li value="64"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What previous research experience do you have? Please feel free to provide references to your work. (Max. 100 words)</span></li><li value="65"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What previous research experience do you have? Prior research experience in fields like ML/CS/math/philosophy may play a role in your ability to do alignment research. That said, if you have done any research in any field that you are proud of, we would also like to hear about that. Feel free to refer to your LinkedIn/resume.</span></li><li value="66"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What's the most awesome thing you've ever done?</span></li><li value="67"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What's your experience with AI safety? If you have any projects or posts, please provide links when able. If you have no experience with AI safety research in particular, please feel free to leave this section blank.</span></li><li value="68"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What’s your motivation for applying to this stream and project? (max 100 words)</span></li><li value="69"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do you want to be part of [Fellowship]? (150 - 300 words) Here you can mention (but are not required to) your personal motivation for AI safety work, why [subfield] research specifically interests you, and how participating now fits your longer-term goals for contributing to the field.</span></li><li value="70"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do you want to work at [Company]?</span></li><li value="71"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do you want to work in AI Safety?</span></li><li value="72"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do you want to work on reducing risks from advanced AI through this fellowship? (1200 characters)</span></li><li value="73"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[50-100 words, max 300] What are 1-3 pieces of evidence that you'd be able to do good research in these streams? (These don't have to be standard credentials!) Please concisely describe them and why they're relevant</span></li><li value="74"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Your Previous Papers &amp; Coding Skills] If applicable, list your past projects/papers, where each line includes (1) link to the paper (published and unpublished writeup), (2) conference and year (if applicable), and (3) part of the code that you programmed (with GitHub links, and notes about which parts you implemented)</span></li><li value="75"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[≤ 150 words] Which topic(s) would you be excited to work on as part of these streams and why? Feel free to select one of the example project ideas provided in the stream descriptions or propose your own.</span></li><li value="76"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[≤ 200 words] Describe a research project that you have done, what went well and some takeaways for improvement.</span></li></ul></div></details><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hope this helps some people make their skills more legible and increases the talent pool that goes towards AI safety. I think communicating your skills is a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hard</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> problem, and often there are little to no mechanisms for feedback when you do poorly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do wish this process weren't so adversarial, to the extent where I have to recommend meta-gaming and reasoning about the </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">grader</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reviewer. I do think there's a better way to structure applications that removes a lot of these inherent problems, but I'm not going to pretend like everyone will "just" get better at job applications.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-1"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The connection between this and LLM meta-gaming is left as a ponderance for the reader. If you are reading this as someone who reviews applications, I'd like to also raise the connection between attempts to clamp down on meta-gaming and LLMs just becoming better at hiding their meta-gaming. Applying for high-status fellowships is inherently a competitive, adversarial environment and if you require applicants to play it you should not be surprised when they succumb to Moloch. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-2"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hello there, powerful person who is reading this. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a real question by the way, completely verbatim. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3:1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-3:2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-4"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You could say they match byte-for-byte. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-PbyxG5EBEEibMmRbS-4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PiP4JqQFKhoqHGG2n/how-to-apply-to-ai-safety-fellowships-and-beyond#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Capabilities research expands the safety-usefulness Pareto frontier too](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nwrx9DHpZfW2Q6zLW/capabilities-research-expands-the-safety-usefulness-pareto)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s tempting to define safety research as research that enables developers to deploy an AI system more safely without making the deployment much more expensive or much less useful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can visualize this definition of safety research as pushing out the </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/efficient-tradeoffs-and-the-safety"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">safety-usefulness Pareto frontier</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At any given level of usefulness, there's greater safety available.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/11de38a045fdd8b48f914e931532716a91cad90e6d941e2b1e3b15880376cf26/muriflqctdfnv7vboi4o" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Awkwardly, this definition counts basically all capabilities research as safety research. For example, consider performance optimization for inference. By making inference more efficient you can use weaker, safer models more extensively than you would otherwise be able to, pushing out the Pareto frontier. Likewise, any successful research whatsoever pushes out this Pareto frontier because research can only ever create more options.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like something has gone wrong with our definition of safety research if it includes seemingly all capabilities research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here, I spell out one reason why enabling improved safety without hurting usefulness is an insufficient standard for safety research. The core observation is that developers have to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">choose a particular point</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the Pareto frontier, and some technological improvements incentivize them to sacrifice safety. Safety research typically reshapes the Pareto frontier in a way that causes developers to choose greater safety, while capabilities research typically does the opposite.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, I also argue there is an important and plausible future regime involving much higher political will than we have right now, in which certain kinds of capabilities research would be an effective way to improve safety. I don't write this post to argue that more people should be doing capabilities research, and in fact I think that currently capabilities research at any frontier AI company is probably bad. Instead, I'm treating this as an interesting puzzle to sharpen our models of when and why safety and capabilities research are good.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Another bucket of safety interventions includes those that cause AI developers to be more willing to give up usefulness for safety. While they’re crucially important, especially right now, I don't focus on those in this post.)</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Buck Shlegeris, Collin Burns, Ryan Greenblatt, Alexa Pan, Bronson Schoen, Thomas Larsen, Oak Hu, Tyler Tracy, Alec Harris, Dylan Xu, Evie Hu, Jackson Sipple, and Jo Jiao for feedback on drafts. Thanks especially to Collin Burns for conversations inspiring some of this thinking.</span></i></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How does research affect the Pareto frontier?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By research, I just mean any work that makes it possible for AI developers to choose new combinations of safety and usefulness when developing and deploying their next model</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs17is69nm29"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns17is69nm29">[1]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5nfopzz1rmm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5nfopzz1rmm">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A tempting gloss is that all research is the same because all research enables greater safety at a given level of usefulness. But this is misleading.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have to actually look at the shape of the Pareto frontier expansion to predict the effects on safety, because ultimately developers are going to choose a particular point on the Pareto frontier, depending on its shape. So, what do capabilities and safety research actually do to the Pareto frontier?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A somewhat more accurate model is that safety research typically creates new options for safety without creating substantial new options for usefulness (pushing the frontier </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">right</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), while capabilities research creates new options for usefulness at the expense of safety without creating substantial new options for safety (pushing the frontier </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">up</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> left</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/e5cf7585f0d7e3f2f6baeb97ef49ea72a14ebdde2a9fe66c0b6449c58fb97923/lhxocjwegka1ghonl4rg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some basic notes about this graph:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety is defined as the probability that the model doesn't take over. I think this is a good definition once AIs start to pose direct takeover risk, but you might want to consider smaller loss-of-control outcomes like rogue internal deployments to quantify the immediate safety of near-term systems (which is correspondingly less important).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can always attain a safety of 1 by not producing or deploying models at all.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The red arrow is not vertical. Models become more likely to take over in substantial part as a result of capabilities. So, when you increase the capabilities of your model, you're typically moving up </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the left on this graph.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety research typically isn’t pure safety either, but the effects on usefulness are more varied (e.g., fixing reward-hacking often improves usefulness, but most methods of improving monitorability degrade usefulness).</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers do not have to be altruistic to care about safety. They don't want to be taken over by the AIs either.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers are irrational in a bunch of ways, and don’t have perfect information, etc (see Appendix B).</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this graph, we can now see why safety tech improvements usually lead to improved safety, while capabilities tech improvements do the opposite.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5b62e677666a358868a55460f692565bca5451cf5c39f7f4e273ef47aaa7fe46/x51a3ngf2znllpa8xeml" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Expanding the safety-usefulness Pareto frontier can reduce the amount of safety that developers choose, by increasing their temptation to increase usefulness</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Indeed, this is the grand trend: risk of AI takeover from AI deployments was historically 0, and in the future it will be greater than 0 because dangerous AI is useful. The particular point on the Pareto frontier that developers choose to go to depends on the developers’ joint utility function over usefulness and safety. This problem is worse for capabilities research than safety research, though in principle it can happen with both (e.g., developing neuralese monitors might tempt rational developers to use neuralese).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety tech improvements will usually lead rational developers to choose higher safety than capabilities tech improvements, because safety tech improvements make the usefulness cost of safety relatively smaller.
