Visually: near the initial pink point, the green slope is shallower than the red slope.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any particular intervention is going to have a somewhat different-looking effect on the shape of the Pareto frontier, but I think that the two above curves capture the first-order difference between safety research and capabilities research. Let’s look at some more detailed examples of capabilities research.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RLVR research that mainly enables improved usefulness, at the expense of safety</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main effect of a lot of capabilities research is enabling developers to make AI much more useful and much more dangerous than was previously possible. Consider building a bunch of RLVR environments, many of which are hackable.  This makes it easier for developers to train a more useful model next time around, but it barely makes it easier for developers to train a safer model. The Pareto frontier expansion might look something like this:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/51fa6408e28c6cc4bb9d9ec3b019eb062c60f9915255881e7d680423d1242171/gwmyzpj6pzxdto8ea6mb" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You'll notice that the lines don't extend all the way to the left. That’s because these hypothetical developers simply don't have the technology to produce a model capable of reliably taking over the world (as is true today). The main effect of the RLVR research was to enable developers to train more capable and dangerous models than they were able to before.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Building more RLVR environments also enables developers to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">slightly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> improve safety for a fixed usefulness. For example, since developers now have a larger pool of RLVR environments to choose from, they can afford to filter out hackable environments more aggressively, without scaling down RL. The resulting model is then trained on higher-quality environments and is therefore somewhat safer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, that’s not what current frontier developers would do. They would choose to train a more capable and dangerous model using most of those new environments.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/80a79a4da7ea6afe11ecb475d5b50d5c3576c424ca4ec54c6090a839a8b45002/vlfo3778jqkrhhvoo7d6" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think all capabilities research looks like this (though I am skeptical), you will think that capabilities research would never be an effective method of improving safety.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inference research that enables somewhat substantial safety improvements</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While building RLVR environments barely creates new options for improving safety, some other capabilities research enables more substantial safety gains.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A simple example is speeding up inference throughput (h/t Buck). At a given level of usefulness, it's probably safer to use a weaker model with more inference-time compute, since we’re more worried about more capable models being competently and egregiously misaligned. While inference-time compute is also dangerous, I’d rather do AI research using 100 parallel instances</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx1zlue8ntp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx1zlue8ntp">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of a model I can probably control and whose alignment I’m more confident in, than 1 instance of an equivalently useful model that doesn't have those properties.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/85685bd442232d87d36a6716ab07e2d6bbe02aa0b27d91e2ecce152ea7cd2bfe/tmerr8h6tjdxj8yecbw3" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Inference speedups can also improve safety via enabling stronger control protocols. Consider a control protocol which resamples suspicious actions. The safety of this protocol depends on how many actions you resample per trajectory. Increasing inference throughput allows you to resample more actions per trajectory at a fixed usefulness, improving safety.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As another example, pretraining and other “predictive” training tasks might be substantially less dangerous than outcome-based RL. At a given final capability</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd00s1mb41u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd00s1mb41u">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/alextmallen/status/2082141491821191553"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stronger pretrains with less RL reward-hack less</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It also seems like</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> current models are substantially more misaligned as a result of flawed RL incentives. Though you might also worry that stronger pretrains are more likely to be unmonitorable or harder to robustly oversee in training. (If you could demonstrate that instead RLVR was safer than pretraining, you could scale up RLVR relative to pretraining instead.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, as I discussed before, merely enabling greater safety at a given level of usefulness does not imply that developers will choose greater safety, and in fact, the opposite is usually true. Both of the above examples also enable the developer to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reduce</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> safety in order to gain more usefulness.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A plausible case in which capabilities research would be an effective safety intervention</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I've discussed, developers need to choose a particular point on the frontier based on what trade-offs they're willing to make between safety and usefulness. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a developer didn't care at all about increasing usefulness above a threshold that they have already achieved, then, in theory, no technological improvements could incentivize them to reduce their safety–there’s nothing to gain from reducing safety. (Alternatively, a developer might be robustly committed to not making safety any worse for whatever reason.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In such a situation, capabilities research would theoretically result in improved safety. For example, in response to pretraining technology improvements, such a developer would do less RLVR training (probably improving safety) while holding capabilities fixed, to whatever extent is enabled by their pretraining technology.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, I think it's implausible that we'll end up in a situation where developers literally don't care at all about increasing usefulness above what they already have, or where the developer is robustly committed to never sacrificing safety. But I think that it's highly plausible that we'll end up in situations similar enough to this that capabilities research might make sense for this reason. Notably, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the key properties of this situation are concentrated in worlds where things go well, since they’re quite related to political will</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. So, to the extent that we're planning for AI to go well, this dynamic is important to keep in mind.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, a common plan is for the leading AI project to “burn their lead” on AI safety once they’ve fully automated AI research. If an AI project actually has a substantial lead they’re willing to burn, this radically changes their decision-making calculus on the safety-usefulness Pareto frontier. They might be willing to massively sacrifice usefulness in order to improve safety, since the calendar time cost would probably be small. Certain kinds of capabilities research might be a big part of the best risk-reducing research portfolio once AIs have just started automating AI research, especially to the extent that capabilities research is more easily automatable than safety research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A similar dynamic applies in certain worlds where AI projects have robustly coordinated internationally to not exceed certain capability thresholds, though not when the slowdown is achieved by limiting inputs to AI development, which leads to continued race dynamics. (One probable implication is that achieving slowdowns via mandating a large fraction of compute goes to transparent safety research has big costs to safety.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Just to be clear again, I think the above argument does not go through right now because developers are still in a tight race, no frontier developer has robustly committed to slowing down when the situation gets too risky, and the downsides of bringing the automation of AI research sooner now are large. I discuss downsides of this proposal in Appendix A and more general inaccuracies of this model in Appendix B.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The issue with the definition at the start of this post—AI safety research is research that enables developers to improve safety without significantly hurting usefulness—is subtle. Just because research can be used to improve safety, doesn't mean it will. You need to pay attention to how research shapes the incentives of the developers. AI safety research should actually enable improved safety without much sacrifice from the developers</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> after taking into account all new options created on the Pareto frontier</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some research, including capabilities research in most cases, creates an incentive for developers to reduce safety to gain more usefulness.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, I’ve argued that if this incentive is absent (e.g., because the developer has already fully automated AI R&amp;D and is properly committed to burn their lead), then certain kinds of safer capabilities research can be an effective way to improve safety. The fact that safety research and capabilities research can blend together in this way is also fairly natural. A classic analogy: when designing an airplane, there's no strong separation between R&amp;D to make airplanes that fly and R&amp;D to make airplanes that don’t crash. Figuring out how to make an airplane that doesn't crash might involve pushing on alternative ways of making an airplane that flies.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A: Reasons why capabilities research might be bad when “burning the lead”</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I proposed that it might be a good idea to do capabilities research to improve safety when “burning the lead”. I expect many people to react that this is heuristically a bad idea. This reaction is worth paying attention to. I expect a bunch of skepticism will come from not buying that AI developers will actually pay for increased safety rather than fooming close to as fast as possible. I am also skeptical about this. But if we focus on good futures, AI projects are moderately likely to intentionally hold back. Ideally, we’d robustly implement the mechanisms for incentivizing capabilities restraint </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> trying to advance capabilities research on this basis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I expect some readers will also just be skeptical that there are any good agendas for increasing capabilities subject to safety constraints because there is fundamentally almost no way to create safe and capable AIs. This is related to a sort of “optimization equivalence” view, in which usefulness just </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> optimization, and all optimization for a given end state will have the same Goodharting problems. I think this view is largely incorrect, and that some capabilities agendas are substantially safer than others (as discussed above).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A final objection is that capabilities research during RSI creates destabilizing capabilities overhangs. If the world later ends up back in a race, e.g., because a blacksite steals the algorithmic progress, it was possibly quite bad that the developers did this capabilities research. They’ve developed a bunch of technology that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">could</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be combined to create a more dangerous AI very suddenly. The severity of concern depends on how much capabilities research was involved in making RSI-capable models that are much safer, which I think people will disagree on a substantial amount. My guess is that this is a small-to-moderate concern because not that much capabilities research will be required, and progress would have been pretty sudden in most late-game equilibrium disruptions anyways.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix B: Further notes about ways in which this model is wrong</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is an incomplete list of considerations missing from my model:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers aren’t rational, don’t have perfect information.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A key concern here is that measurements of safety are extremely hard, and it's very easy to end up in worlds where safety looks good when it's actually bad. Usefulness is comparatively easy to measure, and unfortunately negatively tied to safety (in fact capabilities is probably our single best predictor of danger). This could lead to a situation where a developer that was trying to maintain safety while improving capabilities was unknowingly dragging safety down by scaling up capabilities.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers have other motivations.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Altruism re: not killing everyone</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Altruism re: quickly curing diseases etc</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The joy of building cool things</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fear of losing their job if they're first to highlight safety issues</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intra-developer political dynamics</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not looking evil</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">…</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The “safety” axis only tracks safety from the immediate AI system, not overall risk. This model doesn't capture any of the ways in which developer choices affect the domestic and international political situation, which is currently extremely important. Currently, I think slowing down unilaterally makes a coordinated slowdown more likely.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research builds on other research, in ways that aren't modeled by changing the shape of the Pareto frontier. For example, interpretability research could lead to future research progress on capabilities.</span></li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fns17is69nm29"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs17is69nm29">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this is a distinct definition from Pareto improvements. As we'll see, it's common for AI capabilities research to enable previously unattainable usefulness, at the cost of decreasing safety (often to previously unattainable lows).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5nfopzz1rmm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5nfopzz1rmm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's some modeling choice we have to make here about which options are on the current Pareto frontier versus require expanding the Pareto frontier, and it's pretty subtle. For example, inference speedups make it so that developers can train their model with larger quantities of RL for cheaper. It would potentially require some entrepreneurship / further technological development to implement these speedups in the context of their RL pipeline, build up other necessary complementary capacity, and then figure out how to make use of it, which is why there’s some ambiguity about how to model it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx1zlue8ntp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx1zlue8ntp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note this doesn’t require 100xing inference throughput, since the weaker model is already probably smaller.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd00s1mb41u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd00s1mb41u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whether you measure capability in terms of gold reward or proxy reward the answer is the same.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nwrx9DHpZfW2Q6zLW/capabilities-research-expands-the-safety-usefulness-pareto#comments">Discuss</a>

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a beginner-friendly video. Second half might contain new content for people who already know about acausal trade.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The beginning introduces decision theory and acausal interactions (CDT, EDT, ECL, MBAT).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The middle explains why I think influencing how AIs reason about acausal interactions is time-sensitive.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The last bit talks about how to do the influencing.</span></li></ul><div class="youtube-preview"></div><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eXiP9A5KnkatjAX7y/video-why-acausal-dynamics-are-important#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Lessons from building an automated research scaffold](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zGaQ3SS9C6So9NXFg/lessons-from-building-an-automated-research-scaffold)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We built a scaffold to speed up our own research and gather data on automated alignment research (AAR). It turned out to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be valuable for researcher uplift, but was useful for gathering certain failure modes of AAR. Going forward, we plan to study the broader failure modes of AAR and how these automated research systems can be monitored and analyzed.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’d like to thank Sid Baines, Andrew Draganov, Cameron Holmes and Daniel Tan for helpful comments.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work was carried out by the </span></i><a href="https://www.arcadiaimpact.org/alignment-research" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment Team</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at </span></i><a href="https://www.arcadiaimpact.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arcadia Impact</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in collaboration with Josh Hills, Falko Galperin, and Denis Lim from </span></i><a href="https://www.equistamp.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Equistamp</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and Aleksandr Bowkis from </span></i><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UKAISI</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The scaffold</span></h2><div class="detailsBlockContent"><img alt="ARCH Figure(1).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790937996/lexical_client_uploads/vml42qvxeandnkug7fzl.png" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The scaffold is given a task description and a description of a metric. During setup, an agent writes an evaluation script for the metric and creates a local and held-out evaluation environment. In cases where the task lacks a clear metric, the evaluation consists of LLM judges. Worker agents run on separate VMs and iterate against the metric using only the local environment. An orchestrator monitors their progress by pulling their transcripts, and can restart and steer them. Workers submit their work as pull requests, which are scored on the held-out environment on separate machines.</span></i></p></div></details><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What blocked researcher uplift?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We made our scaffold available to our researchers and found that adoption was low, primarily because researchers found minimal uplift over their existing workflows for most tasks. This was for three main reasons:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our scaffold wasn’t helpful for conceptual work. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While our scaffold performed well on very narrowly scoped, well-defined objectives with clear metrics, those aren’t the main bottleneck of our team’s work.  By the time a project has been reduced to a well-defined metric to hill climb, most of the work is already done. More importantly, our projects rarely take this shape – most of our time goes on thinking through threat models, designing experiments, and analysing results to decide what to run next.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current models have a very spiky skill distribution across automated research tasks. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We find that models are extremely capable at building and running well-specified experiments. However, they are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">very poor</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at interpreting results and deciding what the interesting next research questions are. I.e., it seems like current training methods do not generalise well to research taste and decision-making, especially if the decision requires conceptual clarity.</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since this is a big part of the automated research pipeline, this makes it hard to do end-to-end auto-research.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s easy to fall into scaffold-fixing whack-a-mole. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our initial setup framed the goal of the workers as trying to improve the score, which led to high amounts of reward hacking. We then reframed the task as “doing good science” and specified the metric as an imperfect proxy. This reduced reward hacking frequency, but also made models obsessed with doing ablations and controls on uninteresting things. Once we encountered these problems, we found ourselves bloating system prompts, adding more constraints and components to work around these pitfalls and other model quirks. In doing so, we increased the amount of friction our team faced when trying to use the scaffold, and in many cases the setup cost was not worth the quality of the outputs we got. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The scaffold was useful for gathering failure modes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although our scaffold did not speed up our research as much as we hoped, running it was useful for identifying problems that are likely to matter for automated research more broadly.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Auditing what the agents did is hard, and getting harder.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> After a run, we want to answer three questions: what did the agents actually do, were there any undesired behaviors, did they do good science? We have previously written about how difficult AAR runs are to audit because of the sheer volume of data created and the prose that models adopted (see our previous </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/myAhB5qyAHyXRv6KJ/automated-alignment-runs-are-hard-to-study"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">blogpost</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). With the current trend toward agent swarms, this problem seems likely to </span><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2092692685224325542"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">get substantially harder</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and much more expensive. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is also a bottleneck for live monitoring for misbehavior</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s no ground truth for evaluating monitoring in the AAR regime</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In our transcripts, humans and models often disagree on what counts as misbehavior and which category it falls under. Additionally, since we don’t have good strategies for monitoring multi-agent events, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07368"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">misalignment that encompasses actions across agents can easily be overlooked</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We are therefore planning on building evals for monitoring AAR runs using internal logs and human researcher labels.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, our insights may not transfer beyond the scaffold.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We have very little insight into how labs are doing their automated research work. Even if our scaffold was ‘good’, it may still present different failure modes compared to those which may occur in labs. This is a core reason why we don’t want to over-invest in a single scaffold going forward.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There seem to be many interaction effects between the scaffold and the model's final outputs, such as changes to prompts, roles, components, or objectives, and this makes it difficult to know which findings generalise beyond the scaffold design. We think it’s important to evaluate the model and scaffold together, test a broader class of scaffolds, and study which behaviors persist across them.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next Steps</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are worried that frontier labs will continue </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/an-alien-mind/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">automating AI R&amp;D aggressively</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and that this could further accelerate AI development. At the same time, safety research may be </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06390" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">harder</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://aligned.substack.com/p/crisp-and-fuzzy-tasks?open=false#%C2%A7automated-alignment-research-involves-hard-fuzzy-tasks" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to automate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/04/30/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and fail to keep pace</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We lack a shared understanding of which parts of research are actually automatable, what failure modes there are, and how monitorability changes as we move to larger, longer-horizon multi-agent processes. We therefore want to focus on three things: </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Getting an accurate picture of how automated research is being done. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We strongly think frontier labs should report </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">separately</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> how their capabilities R&amp;D and AI safety research are being automated, including: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which parts of each research process are currently being automated?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much speed-up researchers are actually getting?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are the remaining bottlenecks? </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How are labs monitoring their automated research runs? How do they measure the effectiveness of this monitoring?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if some of this information cannot be made public, we think it’s worth building the best external estimates we can, drawing on safety research organisations outside of the labs, of whether safety automation is keeping pace with capabilities automation. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Building evaluations for monitorability of autoresearch runs. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We currently lack clear-cut methods of measuring whether an autoresearch run went well: whether 1) it was safe and 2) the science produced is valid and useful. We should continuously build stronger ground truth for these judgments, and use it to evaluate and improve model monitors. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Developing proxy settings to study scalable oversight protocols for auto-alignment research.  </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As models become more capable, our ability to oversee and understand their outputs will diminish, so we will need better scalable oversight protocols. Developing and evaluating these directly on automated alignment research is </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PBGKWNrJAbpDgSsPo/obstacles-to-the-scalable-oversight-of-auto-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">extremely difficult</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> because ground truth is expensive to gather. We plan to build proxy settings which are as close as possible to automated alignment research.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zGaQ3SS9C6So9NXFg/lessons-from-building-an-automated-research-scaffold#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Athletic education vs. athletic torment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Rt97G7LpYySgzdcQG/athletic-education-vs-athletic-torment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know if it occurred to me until my thirties to think of exercise as an </span><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.substack.com/p/how-i-love-running"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">enjoyable thing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I was familiar with finding obscure corner-cases that were fun, such as </span><a href="https://meteuphoric.com/2015/04/08/dance-dance-evaluation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dance Dance Revolution</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But the idea of it just often being a good time was alien.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hesitate to blame anyone for anything, but school seems culpable here. I got the impression so firmly that PE class (‘physical education’ or ‘physed’) was a kind of horror, I’m not sure I would have treated this fact as on less solid ground than ‘you’re supposed to put a methods section in your lab report’. It just seemed like the way of things.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For children like me, anyway. Some children liked PE, but those were a totally different kind of creature. What the PE telos included was being awful for nerdy children, maybe to punish them for not being jocklike children. Since nerd children generally like not being jocks, they do not take this punishment as any serious feedback on their way of being, and just nobly withstand it. This is the way it is meant to be, and so it goes for every nerd until they break happily free and never play sports again. So was my rough picture of the situation, if I recall.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So this seems to mark a failure on the part of the schools: couldn’t they have at least got the message to me that this wasn’t the intended narrative, if it wasn’t?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But also, looking at the teaching methods, they don’t seem clearly distinguishable from what one might do if trying to traumatize non-athletic children:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Take all the children, with varying pre-existing levels of athletic skill and mortification about their bodies, and get them to change into different and skimpier outfits in front of each other.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Put them in ‘teams’. This ensures that when one of them does badly at the task, they are letting down half of their classmates (people whose social training so far consists largely of surviving and enacting schoolyard social dynamics, so their handling of this is unlikely to be ideally empathetic and graceful.)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make multiple teams, so while half the class is disappointed by a child’s failure, the other half has a positive stake in it and may cheer the child on contemptuously. This might help teach the child to distrust positivity directed toward them, and respond to praise with stress and doubt rather than accepting it naively.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Traipse the group around outside shouting things and giving some people roles and authorities. Don’t clearly explain anything so that the whole class can hear it. Make reference to things that nerds won’t remember from previous sports classes.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pick a task that is a) physically embarrassing to attempt in front of people if you are unathletic or overweight, b) at a level of difficulty that the least athletic classmates will find impossible and the most athletic will make look extremely easy, and c) allows for a chunk of the class to watch in real time and common knowledge as people fail</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do the activity for a vaguely disclosed period of time. If the nerds seem discouraged or confused, shout things they won’t understand.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Repeat at regular intervals.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Not all of these elements were true in my own education, and probably few all of the time, but I think they are all recognizable.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m guessing from the athletic child standpoint, this is a perfectly reasonable way to be taught that exercise is fun, and the teachers are mostly focused on maintaining that success rather than mitigating the losses with the children who are never going to excel there anyway. But I feel like they could have done so much better, especially if the purpose is in fact to educate the bulk of the students about physical activity, rather than for instance to scout out the student who might make it in professional sports. If it’s not, I think something like that would be cool to have in schools: a class that teaches all students about how to get exercise well and enjoyably.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Rt97G7LpYySgzdcQG/athletic-education-vs-athletic-torment#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI #188: Gemini Dot Argon](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/ai-188-gemini-dot-argon)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Is Google back?</p>
<p>They claim that they are back. Gemini 4 Argon is rolling out, with competitive frontier-level benchmarks, at $2/$10.</p>
<p>What we don’t have is access to the model, because Google Fails Marketing Forever. So it is far too early to say what we have here. When I know more, so will you.</p>
<p>OpenAI was forced to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/astra-61-pulled-as-insufficiently?r=67wny"><strong>pull what would have been GPT-6.1 Astra due to alignment failures</strong></a>. They did offer us GPT-6.1 Sol, which is pitched as approaching Astra quality at the much lower price of $2/$10, the same as Gemini 4 Argon.</p>
<p>The rest of OpenAI’s big Dev Day announcements were Ultrafast mode and Dots, your always-on AI agent based on Astra, which comes with your Pro subscription. I’m trying it out and will report back over time if I find it useful.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25663"></span>


</div>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-opus-55-should-raise-your?r=67wny"><strong>The new hotness remains Claude Opus 5.5</strong></a>. This model rocks. It has made me considerably more productive and made my day more pleasant. It should raise your ambitions. There are some particular reasons to call upon Fable 5.1 or Astra, and sometimes a cheaper model will do, but pending Argon I consider Opus 5.5 my favorite model by a wide margin.</p>
<p>Politics continues. The White House hosted a lunch with the major tech leaders, and everyone <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/a-morally-binding-white-house-accord?r=67wny"><strong>signed a ‘morally binding’ White House accord on AI Safety</strong></a>. This gave the labs the go ahead to meet and agree on safety standards, and also called upon them to complete <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-quest-for-embedded-evaluators?r=67wny"><strong>the Quest for Embedded Evaluators</strong></a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/on-ezra-kleins-podcast-with-jensen?r=67wny"><strong>Jensen Huang went on the Ezra Klein Podcast</strong></a><strong>, </strong>in a conversation worthy of full analysis. Jensen Huang has many deeply unpopular positions, and also expressed many things that are not true, some of which he seems to sincerely believe. Most importantly he thinks of safety and alignment and everything else in AI as engineering problems, which is why he expects the labs to solve the problems. Whereas if the labs can’t solve the problems, suddenly Huang turns into a safety hawk, because he is used to chips where your product has to be safe and work every time.</p>
<p>Tomorrow’s post will cover political developments and the further progress of the preference cascade on AI safety. This includes continued shifts in popular opinion, Congressional hearings, various attempts by the usual suspects to go after anyone and anything that cares about AI safety, and following the money.</p>
<p>Tomorrow I am also going to The Curve, a conference at Lighthaven in Berkeley, arriving Friday afternoon and leaving Monday morning. I am potentially available for high-value meetings with others while I am there, but will have my hands full at the conference.</p>
<p>As usual, I do not write while on trips, and my coverage of breaking news will be on pause until Tuesday. I may or may not queue up some other things in the meantime.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261613/language-models-offer-mundane-utility">Language Models Offer Mundane Utility.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261613/huh-upgrades"><strong>Huh, Upgrades</strong>.</a> Google announces Gemini 4 Argon.</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/217261613/better-call-sol"><strong>Better Call Sol</strong>.</a> Introducing GPT-6.1 Sol.</li>
