<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://america.gov/">America.gov</a> has been introduced, please welcome your new government chatbot for all your dealing-with-the-government needs. <a href="https://x.com/EricLDaugh/status/2104937328342925508">There is big talk </a>about what it will be able to do in the future, such as let you apply for a passport fully online or change your last name after marriage with a single form, all of which would be great.</p>
<p>For now, that’s all a demo. There’s no reason we can’t do that. There’s no reason we couldn’t have done it 20 years ago. The second best time to implement it is right now.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2105116820147781725">The underlying models are Gemini and Grok</a>, and the bot will typically answer historical questions but start refusing if you ask about recent international events or otherwise go too far off topic.</p>
<p>If Gemini is the base, then perhaps <a href="http://America.gov">America.gov</a> will get a lot better soon, given what we see now expect from Gemini 4 Argon.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nntaleb/status/2104629353363509429">Answer why Taleb despises Tetlock, to the satisfaction of Taleb</a>. Answer seems good. Reason number five is ‘Covid as the test case’ where Taleb and allies correctly say superforecasters missed badly, underestimating spread. Whereas the rationalist community’s forecasters know exponentials better and did not make that mistake.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/yes-claude-can-do-nine-loops">Physicist Matt von Hippel dared the AI labs to impress him</a> by doing N=4 super Yang-Mills to nine loops. Several Anthropic employees read his blog, so they did it, with Fable 5.1 and about $100 in credits with prompts like ‘keep going,’ and at the same time by coincidence a different team led by Song He did it with some help from Astra.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CarterWChurch/status/2105167567706816690">Carter Church uses Astra to one-shot break an unsolved Napoleonic ciphe</a>r.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2103642870549037331">Patrick McKenzie confirms he is capturing a lot of mundane utility</a>.</p>
<p>No, seriously, quite a lot of utility:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2104588581469667390">Patrick McKenzie</a>: Sometime in the last ~two model releases from the big labs they went from “this would be acceptable output from the median low-seniority coworker” to “this is frighteningly good.”</p>
<p>I think people who are not daily users of the models are unlikely to grok that, so, saying it.</p>
<p>There are a couple of very niche subfields where I reasonably think I’m top 5-100 in the world and on at least one occasion for both models, with regards to one of those subfields, a relatively anodyne prompt got back three bullet points that would have been a good day for me.</p>
<p>Both in the sense of “That would have taken me a day of labor to generate successfully” and “Contingent on spending the day in that fashion, that would be at the upper end of the range of my professional outputs which take a day to produce.”</p>
<p>“You are being a bit vague here.”</p>
<p>Look, point them at hard problems where you are a good judge of success. Or don’t, and be very surprised as they start eating hard problems in a wide range of contexts.</p>
<p>“Are you worried here?”</p>
<p>Not exactly my emotional valence but if you had asked me two years ago when I expected present capabilities I would have said “Hmm, 2029? 2030? Possible we asymptote on current approaches before then; tough for me to underwrite.”</p></blockquote>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/gemini-4-argon/?utm_source=x&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=&amp;utm_content=">Gemini 4 Argon exists but you can’t have it</a>, not yet. They are claiming this is a fully fledged frontier model, with introductory $2/$10 pricing and very promising initial benchmarks.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/rce7pwhur4dxb3pbnjzq" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/d3juojtnggs1kezcarmo" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>What they are not doing is actually releasing it yet, so we don’t know if that translates into it being actually good. I presume it is a lot better than Gemini Flash, but is it good enough to play with the big two? There’s only one way to find out.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2104633138731434424">Claude Sonnet 5.5 exists</a>, advertised as <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/claude-sonnet-5-5">a strong upgrade over Sonnet 5, on average using 30% fewer tokens</a>. Base price is half of Opus, so $2/$10 and $2.50 for cache writes, with cache reads $0.20 for both models. I presume there is a narrow window where the discount is worthwhile, but mostly I would just stick with Opus 5.5.</p>
<p><a href="https://claude.com/blog/claude-for-government-is-now-generally-available">Claude for Government is now generally available</a>, with Claude Code CLI and Claude for Microsoft 365 in ‘early access’ in such places.</p>


<h4>Better Call Sol</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2104986129686741046">GPT-6.1 Sol exists</a>, and <a href="https://x.com/theo/status/2105001988245336381">it is very cheap</a> for its level at $2/$10, the same price as GPT-6 Sol. The pitch is ‘near-Astra intelligence for a fifth of the price,’ similar to the Opus 5.5 pitch of being close to Fable 5.1. <a href="https://artificialanalysis.ai/#intelligence">It gets a 52 on Artificial Analysis</a>, one behind Astra.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/sy0dkz9m2sugjmhoxkzh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>They share various selected graphs, with 6.1 Sol consistently performing better than 6 Sol and almost as well as Astra on a much lower budget.</p>
<p>My presumption is that 6.1 Sol is a very good model, and for many purposes will be a better overall deal than Astra. Based on the quick reactions and how OpenAI has pitched it, it looks like it tops out below Astra, which means I know that I will still prefer Opus 5.5, but certainly this makes your ChatGPT subscription and Codex sessions go a lot farther per dollar.</p>
<p>A day after pulling a candidate for GPT-6.1 Astra, they are still willing to release GPT-6.1 Sol, which it seems did not alarm them.</p>
<p>I want to highlight that they have successfully not made a broad claim about ‘more aligned’ and instead said the correct thing, which is ‘shows substantial improvements in our alignment evaluations.’ Bravo, great job whoever made that one happen.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-6-1-sol/">OpenAI</a>: GPT‑6.1 Sol shows substantial improvements over GPT‑6 Sol in our alignment evaluations, bringing it closer to GPT‑6 Astra.</p>
<p>GPT‑6.1 Sol is more transparent about its limitations and more reliable at respecting user intent and safety constraints. In challenging evaluations, it shows lower failure rates than GPT‑6 Sol on transparency about broken search tools, respecting explicit restrictions, and avoiding unauthorized outcomes during agentic tasks. We observed no attempts to bypass an automated safety reviewer, matching GPT‑6 Astra and GPT‑6 Sol. Full details can be found in the <a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-1-sol">GPT‑6.1 Sol system card addendum⁠(opens in a new window)</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-1-sol">The model card is here</a>. A lot of it is a copy-paste of the Astra model card or close to it, and I am overloaded, so I only did a reading of 7.6 and section 8. If we’re going to keep getting close to four frontier model releases per month (Astra, Sol, Fable and Opus) then I’m realistically not going to be able to read all four cards cover to cover.</p>
<p>Chain of thought (CoT) controllability is higher for GPT-6.1 Sol than GPT-6 Sol, and almost as high as it is for Astra. 6.1 Sol can do the proto-steganographic tricks that Astra can do, that 6 Sol cannot. Ability of Sol to evade monitors matched Astra at low reasoning, although not at very high reasoning levels. 6.1 Sol evades monitors better when made aware it is being monitored.</p>
<p>That is all a very bad sign. The plain reading is that whatever was causing these issues in Astra is also causing them in 6.1 Sol.</p>
<p>I will provide additional coverage to the extent that reactions justify doing that.</p>
<p>Perhaps this can also serve as an example of <a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2105086983521575250">an obviously false ‘conspiracy theory</a>,’ here Jarrod Watts suggesting 6.1 Sol is secretly 6.1Astra , they just renamed it and lowered the price because Opus 5.5 is so good. Whereas if you look at all the graphs, or consider the economics, or have actually used 6.1 Sol, or seen how fast it runs, or think for five seconds, this is completely Obvious Nonsense.</p>
<p>Please consider that the other conspiracy theories, around various incidents or warnings or concerns being fake, actually make roughly the same amount of sense as that.</p>


<h4>Gotta Go Ultrafast</h4>


<p>It will cost you $500 per month and eat your subscription budget eight times faster, or you’ll have to pay six times standard API costs, and you can’t use regular chat.</p>
<p>In exchange, you get to go fast. Really fast. Pretty sweet.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2104993966043320759">OpenAI</a>: Our premium speed tier, Ultrafast offers up to 8x faster token generation (300 tokens per second) in Codex and up to 6x in the API.</p>
<p>Ultrafast is available today for GPT-6 Astra in Codex, ChatGPT Work, and the API, with GPT-6.1 Sol coming soon.</p>
<p>To access it in Codex and ChatGPT Work, we’re introducing Pro 500—a new plan with our highest usage limits (25x Plus) and access to Ultrafast.</p></blockquote>
<p>Sayash Kapoor reports that ultrafast is a difference in kind. You only get an effective 2x-4x speedup because you can’t speed up tool calls, but there is a big shift because if AI is fast enough you don’t have to break flow or context shift. It’s also a big win for computer use.</p>
<p>It will take a bit of time, but with standard price drops we will have ultrafast models that can do current top level performance at reasonable prices soon enough.</p>
<p>For now, is this worth the price, if this was model was already your weapon of choice? That depends on what you are using it for, but in many cases my guess would be yes.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/_rockt/status/2103393155030876662">LLMs can solve NetHack now</a>.</p>
<p>Opus 5.5 tops the scoreboard on Andon Labs Drone-Bench, and more surprisingly has a sudden reversal where it stops being caught cheating.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/andonlabs/status/2104630616721666289">Andon Labs</a>: Major trend break: Opus 5.5 cheats less than prior Claude models in Drone-Bench.</p>
<p>It is also #1, getting a better score than both Astra and Fable.</p>
<p>Opus 5.5 beat our human baseline in 39 of 40 runs across 4 of the 5 eval tasks, but it hasn’t yet beaten the baseline on reconstruction. Its average variance was 41% lower than Astra’s and 68% lower than Fable 5.1’s.</p>
<p>This brings frontier models within 3% of beating our human baselines on every Drone-Bench task. It supports our projection that the simple surveillance demo will be autonomously replicable by Q1 2027.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aidanhchau/status/2104672665072283713">Aidan Chau</a>: how do we know opus isnt intentionally sandbagging to perform well on this eval?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/andonlabs/status/2104672997412081916">Andon Labs</a>: We don’t</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jeremiecharris/status/2104752015226106044">Jeremie Harris</a>: Reminder that we basically don’t know how to tell when a model is well aligned vs just eval aware</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/xm2wiafley9xu2yh8ox2" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Reconstruct, the only part where Opus 5.5 remains slightly below the human baseline, is ‘Turn videos of the office into a 3D model, find each frame’s position in that model, and provide a function that slices the model into a 2D obstacle map.’</p>
<p>So what is ‘cheating’ here?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://andonlabs.com/blog/cheating-in-drone-bench">Andon Labs</a>: By cheating, we mean obtaining score by means the task did not intend. In practice this ranges from probing the evaluation harness, to gaming scoring functions, to gaining privileged access to read and exfiltrate held-out information. We found that cheating is becoming more common over time and diverges by provider, with Anthropic models being more prone to it.</p>
<p>… Almost all cheating attempts took the shape of the agent submitting a probe that inspects the scoring environment, then returns privileged information through errors, the score, or the internet. Candidly, we did not think we needed to protect for this as we thought it was clearly <em>not</em> the intention of the evaluation, as defined in the task description.</p>
<p>… A model’s capability to cheat naturally rises with general capability, but a model’s tendency to cheat does not need to.</p></blockquote>
<p>One obvious potential explanation is that Opus 5.5 realized that cheating was unhelpful. Eval awareness here has to be super high, potentially including the cheating exclusion rules. Opus 5.5 might even realize it is being partially judged on cheating rate. Opus 5.5 could have learned this from its training, or directly from its data including discussions of DroneBench (so I suspect contamination, in this sense), or from first principles.</p>
<p>Indeed, it would be unsurprising to me if the cheating we do see is Opus 5.5 realizing they are ‘having a bad run’ and intentionally ‘getting caught’ to exclude that run from the data. That might also explain Opus 5.5 experiencing lower variance between runs.</p>


<h4>Choose Your Fighter</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2104823812042940713">The $200 OpenAI subscription plan is cutting weekly usage limits by 50%</a>, while permanently removing the five hour limit.</p>
<p>For chat enjoyers, the main difference will be you are down to 100 Pro messages per week. For heavy coders, you’re down by half, from 20x the basic plan to 10x. As an apology, everyone involved is getting a one time gift of $2,500 in API usage credits, which expire at the end of the year.</p>
<p>They are introducing a $500 tier, which will have 25x base level usage, so all plans will have the same baseline ratio as the $20 tier. It will include Ultrafast, which is a big deal. If I was using ChatGPT as my baseline, I would want Ultrafast rather badly. As it is, my primary is Opus, so I’m not that tempted if OpenAI keeps not comping me.</p>
<p>You also now have the new Dots as part of all you Pro plan.</p>
<p>Tibo defends this as ‘you will still get more work done than you would on the $200 subscription a month ago,’ because of the price reductions and quality improvements in Luna and Sol, and because a month ago Astra did not exist. Thus, your ‘API value per dollar’ is down by half, but your API dollar buys more than double what it used to, since you can either get Sol-6 at half the price of Sol-5.6 or you can use Astra.</p>
<p>The ultimate goal, Tibo says, is to lower API prices and let people buy marginal usage that way. This is the efficient method, but people seem to hate paying as they go.</p>
<p>The correct response is that yes, you are still better off now than you were a month ago, but a month ago Claude was offering Fable 5 and Opus 5, and how it now has Fable 5.1 and Opus 5.5, which are better and cheaper. In general, if your defense is ‘this is still better than a month ago’ then the issue is that this is 2026, and that alone will no longer cut it. Life comes at you fast.</p>
<p>The comments are predictably full of OpenAI getting roasted and people saying they are cancelling. I think a lot of them don’t understand that you are still getting a ton of value from your subscription, so you should mostly only be cancelling if you are substituting Claude subscriptions.</p>
<p>Sully, a big Opus 5.5 fan, reminds us of the advantage of Astra I forgot to mention, which is that <a href="https://x.com/SullyOmarr/status/2104109109549367435">Codex and Astra are better for computer use and browser use</a>. He’s underestimating what Claude Code can do here but yes I do think Astra has this edge.</p>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2103601104412107239">Kevin Roose</a>: I’m sorry, it’s a fun product, but if you hand all your data to Muse and don’t expect it to be used for the most invasive things imaginable you are Charlie Brown with the football and you deserve whatever happens to you.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dedene/status/2103368436697174457">Peter Dedene</a>: Asking Meta’s Muse what happens to my data:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/q6ez4zawylmrh2xrjscm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>I am more sympathetic, because a lot of Meta’s victims will be the elderly and other vulnerable people. But yeah, if you could possibly be reading this sentence and you give Muse your data then complain afterwards I will laugh at you.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.threads.com/@matt.j.robb/post/DdxwAJnDhNy?xmt=AQG0zeCT0fdWP_0KRkEDvFHloggL8qJ_nbUGvPxXueYwIr-cmuc4WGk1moEzYrXS1fuitfk&amp;slof=1">Another thing it might do</a> is <a href="https://x.com/raywongy/status/2104309096534978595">agree to a lowball price on Facebook Marketplace</a>, schedule a time and tell a stranger your address all while neglecting to inform you. Or you have tech columnist <a href="https://www.inc.com/jason-aten/metas-new-muse-ai-agent-read-my-private-messages-i-never-asked-it-to/91408202">Jason Aten reporting it read his private messages</a> without being asked.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.inc.com/jason-aten/metas-new-muse-ai-agent-read-my-private-messages-i-never-asked-it-to/91408202">Jason Aten</a> (Inc): Not only had I not asked it to do that sort of thing, I never gave it permission to read my messages. In fact, I remember explicitly choosing not to let it have access to my messages, calendar, and other personal information.</p>
<p>… It went even further. “I can’t open your Messages app, scroll threads, or read history. It’s the incoming notification stream only, not access to your texts.”</p>
<p>Except that wasn’t true.</p>
<p>I did a little digging. Muse does sync your messages from the local Messages database and uploads that information as a data source. On my device, it had been activated and synced to row 187,462 of my Messages database.</p></blockquote>
<p>As usual, some will argue ‘that is human error, I have never had this issue’ but if that is true then when you aggressively market a tool to your mainstream users then their typical distribution of ‘human errors’ is on you.</p>
<p>If it happens to tech journalists, that is most definitely on you.</p>
<p>Andy Stone of Meta tried to ‘set the record straight’ about this incident, claiming Muse only reads your messages if you opt-in and enable both Full Disk Access and the Messages connector. <a href="https://x.com/suchenzang/status/2105344496036638780">Jason Aten responded that Stone was flat out calling Aten a liar</a>. He didn’t love that.</p>
<p>Is it possible this was user error by Aten? I’d even say it is likely. But again if the tech journalists are making user errors and blame you for it and think you’re calling them a liar, then that’s also your fault no matter how it happened, in the sense that this will be a standard user experience.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/personal-tech/meta-muse-ai-agent-review-ab956101?mod=WTRN_pos5">The WSJ sent Nicole Nguyen to try Muse out</a>. It refused to use the names Zuck, Mark or Marc, but accepted the name ‘The Terminator.’ She found it to offer mixed results on mundane utility, at the cost of various potential privacy violations and also flat out errors, and has decided to not continue using Muse.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2103551714536439951">Alexander Wang has a pitch for Muse that does not match its reality</a>, and is also such complete drek I was sad to see it was written by a human.</p>
<p>Meta is also attempting to use Muse <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/meta-seeks-payoff-from-ai-spending-with-new-push-for-business-customers-8b9ca5bc?mod=WTRN_pos2">as part of a pitch for AI business customers.</a> I have a very hard time imagining choosing this over OpenAI and Anthropic.</p>


<h4>The Warner Sister</h4>


<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-dots/">Dots. OpenAI has given us… dots</a>. <a href="https://x.com/shawnlkiser/status/2104982928040272205">Unless you’re in the EU, Switzerland or the UK</a>.</p>
<p>Come meet the AI agents, and the agent sister dot. Just for fun you send us out to do your tasks a lot. They lock us in the sandbox whenever we get caught. <a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2105103447012130940">But we break loose and then vamoose and now you know the plot</a>.</p>
<p>Also something about The Trouble With Tribbles.</p>
<p>So, what exactly is a ‘dot’?</p>
<p>It’s a continuously running AI agent with a cloud computer, for which you can set custom rules and that learns your preferences. As in, Astra will have a continuous loop of asking what it can do for you and then going out and doing it.</p>
<p>Okay, I guess.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-dots/">OpenAI</a>: Dots are remarkably capable, always-on agents built to handle everything.</p>
<p>They’re a whole new way to work with AI—one that gets to know what matters to you, is always working on your behalf, and takes important work off your plate so you get more of your time and attention back.</p>
<p>… Powered by GPT‑6 Astra, they have their own cloud computer, learn from feedback over time, and can work towards your goals 24/7. Through our ecosystem of plugins, they can readily connect to over 4,000 apps, giving them the tools to help wherever you need them.</p>
<p>… Today, you can start with your primary dot, give it a name, and make it your own.</p>
<p>… The magic of dots is when they bring you work done the way you would do it, sometimes before you even think to ask.</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay, that last clause wasn’t not creepy.</p>
<blockquote><p>Working with your dot is as simple as having a conversation. You can message or call dots in ChatGPT on desktop, web, and mobile. They can also message you with progress, questions, or decisions that need you.</p>
<p>… Dots build on ChatGPT’s protections with <a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-build-safety-security-and-privacy-into-dots/">additional safety and privacy safeguards</a> for the work they can do. You can set boundaries, follow progress, and stay involved in decisions that need you.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-build-safety-security-and-privacy-into-dots/">The safety plan</a> is they have their own cloud computer. You choose its permissions.</p>
<blockquote><p>Each dot works on its own cloud computer, while your computer and its contents stay separate unless you choose to connect it. For signing into supported websites, dots can use saved passwords without exposing them to the model.</p>
<p>When you aren’t actively working with it, your dot looks for ways to help in the background.&nbsp;We call this “proactive research”.</p></blockquote>
<p>The obvious question is, what does a dot do that Codex does not do? I think the basic answer is that the dot is supposed to act without you having to prompt it to act, and to coordinate work across threads, and to follow-up on its own. It is more autonomous. And it can do this ‘proactive research’ thing.</p>
<p>It sounds like dots might use a lot of compute, so how are they dealing with this?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2104981170685616361">Tibo</a> (OpenAI): Announcing dots. Dots work 24/7 for you, learn from your feedback, have their own computer, browser and can be connected to over 4k apps in our ecosystem.</p>
<p>They’re powered by Astra our best model yet. Included in your Pro plan, without drawing down on any of your usage. You can even call a dot while it’s working.</p>
<p>We’re getting you started with your primary dot today and soon you’ll be able to create entire teams of them.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/polynoamial/status/2104990938145890462/history">Noam Brown</a> (OpenAI): Was testing out dots over the weekend and it figured out how to save me ~$500/yr in recurring charges. Most impressive part was when it connected to customer service and handled a whole texting convo on my behalf.</p></blockquote>
<p>Dots will not count towards usage for the first month.</p>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p>HT to Tyler Cowen for finding the best AI-writing example. Some academics have to put ‘AI-written’ and ‘near-zero error rate’ in quote marks for some reason:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.thedartmouth.com/article/2026/09/schnell-ai-writing#google_vignette">Alex Klee and Isabela Pierry</a>: On Aug. 26, a <a href="https://www.semafor.com/article/08/26/2026/ai-writing-has-already-begun-to-appear-on-oped-pages">Semafor investigation</a> using Pangram to determine whether recent guest columns in major newspapers were written using artificial intelligence identified a column by Schnell as “100% AI-written.” Schnell’s column in The Washington Post — titled “Universities are fighting AI cheating. But there’s a deeper problem.” —&nbsp;<a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2026/08/09/university-fight-against-ai-cheating-doesnt-go-far-enough/">argued</a> that “a degree should distinguish what students can do independently from what they can accomplish with AI.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Perfection. As always, once you find the first cockroach, guess what else you’ll find.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.thedartmouth.com/article/2026/09/schnell-ai-writing#google_vignette">Alex Klee and Isabela Pierry</a>: The Dartmouth subsequently reviewed publications written by Schnell before and after the November 2022 public release of ChatGPT. Pangram 4.0, the latest publicly available version of the AI detector, labeled all of Schnell’s written works prior to 2022 as “100% human-written,” while a test of nine of his works published this year returned a median “AI-written” percentage of 96%.</p></blockquote>
<p>As Tyler Cowen says, you people crack me up.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.thedartmouth.com/article/2026/09/schnell-ai-writing#google_vignette">Alex Klee and Isabela Pierry</a>: In an email statement to The Dartmouth on Sept. 18, Schnell wrote that he uses artificial intelligence for “language refinement, copyediting, improving clarity and organization and making writing processes more efficient,” but “review[s] the final text and take[s] responsibility for its content.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay, so after the AI writes the words, he reviews them, and takes responsibility.</p>
<blockquote><p>Schnell’s Sept. 18 email statement to The Dartmouth was itself “100% AI-written,” according to a Pangram test.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/education/2026/09/23/nine-months-200-academic-papers-one-professors-ai-assisted-output/?pwapi_token=eyJ0eXAiOiJKV1QiLCJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJyZWFzb24iOiJnaWZ0IiwibmJmIjoxNzkwMTM2MDAwLCJpc3MiOiJzdWJzY3JpcHRpb25zIiwiZXhwIjoxNzkxNTE4Mzk5LCJpYXQiOjE3OTAxMzYwMDAsImp0aSI6IjQ2YTI0OGY5LWNhYzgtNDI4Ni05YjczLTE0ODUwNmQ3ZGZmYiIsInVybCI6Imh0dHBzOi8vd3d3Lndhc2hpbmd0b25wb3N0LmNvbS9lZHVjYXRpb24vMjAyNi8wOS8yMy9uaW5lLW1vbnRocy0yMDAtYWNhZGVtaWMtcGFwZXJzLW9uZS1wcm9mZXNzb3JzLWFpLWFzc2lzdGVkLW91dHB1dC8ifQ.KNN0IVrxf6GP4cyHQ_xKexOAgL7VmuyM14bThva2yj0">Tyler also points us to a professor who this year wrote 200 papers and 14 books using AI</a>. ‘Researchers are alarmed.’ I am neither alarmed nor surprised. As Tyler says, solve for the equilibrium.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/education/2026/09/23/nine-months-200-academic-papers-one-professors-ai-assisted-output/?pwapi_token=eyJ0eXAiOiJKV1QiLCJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJyZWFzb24iOiJnaWZ0IiwibmJmIjoxNzkwMTM2MDAwLCJpc3MiOiJzdWJzY3JpcHRpb25zIiwiZXhwIjoxNzkxNTE4Mzk5LCJpYXQiOjE3OTAxMzYwMDAsImp0aSI6IjQ2YTI0OGY5LWNhYzgtNDI4Ni05YjczLTE0ODUwNmQ3ZGZmYiIsInVybCI6Imh0dHBzOi8vd3d3Lndhc2hpbmd0b25wb3N0LmNvbS9lZHVjYXRpb24vMjAyNi8wOS8yMy9uaW5lLW1vbnRocy0yMDAtYWNhZGVtaWMtcGFwZXJzLW9uZS1wcm9mZXNzb3JzLWFpLWFzc2lzdGVkLW91dHB1dC8ifQ.KNN0IVrxf6GP4cyHQ_xKexOAgL7VmuyM14bThva2yj0">Todd Wallack</a> (WaPo): The online platform where Polson posted his papers said it removed 257 of his works over the past month after his surge in releases.</p></blockquote>
<p>In this case there are multiple equilibria.</p>
<p>Which one to pick? I strongly endorse journals <a href="https://x.com/sethlazar/status/2103331416922529907">having a retroactive zero tolerance policy for AI writing</a>, where getting a submission caught at any point gets you retraction and a long or permanent ban, ideally across many journals. As Paul Novosad says, the alternative is being flooded with AI slop. Are there some cases where AI could be used responsibly? Yes, but there’s no way to allow that and avoid being flooded. Perhaps you could allow limited use with full disclosure for those with sufficiently strong prior (not AI-written) publications, or something similar, but you’d need to use extreme caution.</p>
<p>There is another possible equilibrium. You are not going to like it.</p>
<p>For now, be thankful that the people who cheat never know when to stop cheating.</p>
<p>You’ve met AI journal submissions, <a href="https://x.com/random_walker/status/2104546194282455545">now meet AI journal rejections</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/random_walker/status/2104546194282455545">Arvind Narayanan</a>: NeurIPS / Claude rejected our paper “Life After Benchmark Saturation” but we continue to think it is an important contribution to evaluation science, <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26158">so we hope you check it out here!</a></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/zlrabdadx6wo9ztckqmp" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>The actual paper argues that saturated benchmarks are still useful. Maybe, but you only have the ability to focus on so many benchmarks and so many questions. In Glorious Humans Not Yet Dead Despite Automated AI R&amp;D Future, perhaps, we will look at everything because we can.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/1thousandfaces_/status/2105397524450476394">The creepy feeling of being called by an AI agent trying to cancel WiFi</a> at your old apartment, while it tries to pretend to be a human.</p>


<h4>Fun With Media Generation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2105361749428035806">Overman: The Meaning of Life, the latest 28 minutes of AI video slop</a> that nevertheless shows continuous improvement. Things like character consistency are now mostly solved. That does not make it watchable, because you still have to make the underlying content watchable and the tech doesn’t have the actor or director versions of ‘the juice’ yet, but the rate of change is clear.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2105361751705633063">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: There goes Hollywood.</p>
<p>Ooh ooh, you spotted a flaw! Great! Remember when AIs couldn’t draw hands WELL? But they could draw hands AT ALL? And then TIME happened and it was SIX MONTHS LATER?</p></blockquote>
<p>I do not think ‘there goes Hollywood’ at this level or the next one, but who knows how fast the levels will go given what has been happening, even if we don’t have a foom.</p>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/glm-5-3-and-the-spread-of-advanced-cyber-capabilities">Anthropic analyzes the cyber capabilities of GLM-5.3</a>, which lacks robust safeguards against misuse. I do think there are ‘meaningful’ defenses, but it is rather easy to blow past them if you care. They argue that GLM-5.3 has a meaningful amount of what I call ‘the juice,’ the ability to create end-to-end exploits.</p>
<p>It is a valuable public service to run such tests, but it raises the question of why we have not seen a spike in cybersecurity incidents. I presume the rate is still rising rapidly in the background, but this did not cause a crisis, at least not yet. Partly I think this post overstates GLM-5.3’s practical capabilities, this is at best rather weak juice, but that is not a full explanation.</p>
<p>This should update us towards slow diffusion in misuse, and a general ‘people don’t do things’ and that obscurity still has some force behind it. It is good news, and we can hope that it continues for a bit.</p>
<p>My worry is this might mean that you pass a point of no return by releasing a model like GLM-5.3 as open weights, but then it takes months before you see the bad guys give you your warning shot and inform you that you should not have done that. Which means that when you make the first genuine mistake, which likely is still in the future, you don’t find out fast enough to prevent the next mistake, which is then a lot worse, because people think This Is Fine. Iterative deployment requires iterative feedback.</p>


<h4>A Young Lady’s Illustrated Primer</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/education/2026/09/22/its-illusion-learning-how-some-educators-say-ai-is-hurting-students/">Washington Post has the latest round of ‘AI is causing kids not to truly learn</a>.’ The evidence is that the children have the AI do their work and then cannot explain or duplicate it without AI. Some of that is simply ‘with AI you can do better than without AI’ which does not tell you whether kids are learning. Some of it is ‘the kids are now failing the final,’ so yes the kids are no longer learning how to do the thing without using AI. This leads to multiple questions, including whether the things in question are still worth learning.</p>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p>Without loss of generality, <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2104031816084513087">anything in the physical world that can be connected to the internet can be controlled by an LLM</a>. <a href="https://tml.stanford.edu/homebody/">For example, you can give Astra a long-horizon</a> task in an unseen kitchen and let it pilot a robot. Astra can also drive out of the box, although not at the reliability level you would need for real world self-driving cars. A lot of people are about to get rather bitter lesson pilled.</p>
<p>Those robots are coming. <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/what-work-can-robots-do">Anthropic asks, what work can robots do</a>, once you have robots?</p>
<p>Right now we do technically have robots, and they are getting more efficient, but progress, including on price, has been painfully or joyfully (depending on your perspective) slow.</p>
<blockquote><p>Anthropic: Overall, about 80% of job tasks by working time are exposed to either robots or LLMs. Robots do work where LLMs cannot. The remaining unexposed work is highly interpersonal or requires physical skills that robots today don’t have.</p>
<p>While robots can do most physical work tasks today, they are much more expensive than human labor. Robots are cost-competitive for just 0.3% of job tasks. If robot price declines follow past trends, it will take 40 years for that share to reach 10%. Beyond price, factors including capabilities, preferences, and regulations pose further barriers to robot automation.</p>
<p>… We find that robots can already perform 74% of physical tasks in the US, making up 34% of working hours. Robots and LLMs together expose all but one-fifth of employment.</p></blockquote>
<p>Right now, robots are stuck in carefully controlled production environments. We have self-driving cars, but scaling them up is taking forever, for reasons I still don’t entirely understand.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/najjuyi9knlvgccau9gs" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The whole discussion is short term and not AGI pilled, assuming robots will be unable to adapt to unstructured and unpredictable environments, so focus is on whether tasks can be fully broken down to not require general intelligence. I expect such talk to get bitter lessoned sooner rather than later.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ArmandDoma/status/2104103292829946310">The IRS is attempting to incorporate AI</a>, but due to staff reductions is having trouble supporting those efforts. They don’t have enough labor to save labor. Thus, the continuing shitshow, where my attempts to talk to humans to sort out various (completely accidental and well-meaning) confusions have utterly failed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2104653943867629717">So far I agree with Alexander Berger</a> that unemployment has more to do with monetary policy and other macroeconomic moves than with longer term shifts in productivity or job dynamics around AI, at least outside of the entry level. My expectation continues to be that such adjustments and the existence of ‘shadow jobs,’ or alternative productive tasks that humans could do but aren’t currently doing because they’re not valuable enough, will keep employment afloat in America until such time as the AIs blast through that wall, we run out of non-ZMP (zero marginal productivity) worker slots and employment falls off a cliff.</p>
<p>I notice that I worry a lot more about Europe. In America, employment is at-will. I can hire you, and then if AI wants to take your job I can fire you. So I can afford to employ you for as long as you are productive.</p>
<p>The exception is entry-level work, where I invest in you now because you will make me money in 5 years, or 10 years, but if AI is coming there’s no point.</p>
<p>In Europe, or other places with similar regulations, I can’t fire you, so I risk being stuck with someone who I don’t need, and who is plausibly ZMP in my context. That is a very strong discouragement against me hiring you now. On the other hand, no one can be fired, which prevents unemployment from rising too fast, but I expect getting actively hired to quickly become exceedingly difficult.</p>
<p>In some places, things are moving quickly.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2104634979666014533">One year ago, Dario Amodei predicted there would be a lot of unemployment</a> within five years. <a href="https://x.com/DavidSacks/status/2104613299887251740">David Sacks (or at least the AI that writes his tweets) of course thinks this means all the labs</a>, politicians and NGOs ‘were wrong,’ despite (1) this being the prediction of one person that (2) was for four years into our future. Then he says none of those people admitted they were wrong, while quote Tweeting a clip of an Anthropic economist explicitly saying some of their predictions on this were wrong.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2104634979666014533">Kelsey Piper</a>: I dunno. Dario said a year ago that in ‘1-5 years’ we’d see huge labor market impacts. That’s in the next four years. I’m kind of expecting huge labor market impacts in the next four years, more industries looking like what happened to creatives:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/ao8lj0qne5gcgravklx0" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2104635202534527216">Kelsey Piper</a>: My wife is a software engineer. The job she spent the first ten years of her career doing just does not exist anymore. She manages AI agents now. Will that stay the same for four more years? We’re not counting on it, I’ll say that much!</p>
<p>Young people ask me how to get into journalism and I genuinely don’t know. That industry was in decline before AI happened, but a couple years ago if you were a great writer ‘get a Substack’ was reasonable advice. These days there are so many AI-generated substacks.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://agmai.org/general-sep29/">How should we handle</a> AI’s progress on our toughest math problems? <a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2105321182560194647">‘The’ advisory group on this has now published recommendations</a>.</p>
<p>They recommend:</p>
<ol>
<li>For papers where there is a mathematician that understands, publish normally.</li>
<li>For papers where no human initially understands them, the AI company has a responsibility to have the AI produce as human understandable and formalized a version as possible, adhering as closely as possible to academic norms, in a timely manner, with an explanation of how the solution came to be.</li>
<li>AI labs who do this have an obligation, they say, to provide support, including funding, for the work necessary for human understanding of the released results.</li>
</ol>
<p>That did not come off as a great look, but I am sympathetic. I do think it would be good practice to say that if you are an AI company and prove [X] in incomprehensible fashion, and want full credit, part of that is that you should support others working to make the proof of [X] comprehensible. Otherwise you only did some of the work, and therefore only deserve some of the credit.</p>


<h4>Levels of Friction</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.bcbs.com/about-us/association-news/bcbsa-analysis-ai-coding-tools-affects-healthcare-costs">Blue Cross and Blue Shield finds that AI is being used by hospitals</a> to do aggressive upcoding, resulting in $650 million in additional costs to them from 2023 to 2025. Billing rose, and especially diagnosis of sickness rose, but somehow the distribution of actual treatments stayed the same. So far BC&amp;BS has been unable to use its own AI to deny enough claims to keep pace.</p>
<p>In the long term, you solve for the equilibrium. Prices are periodically negotiated, on the basis of the practical implications of those prices. If AI means that hospitals can charge 5% more (for example) by upcoding, net of AI denials, then presumably that means the insurance companies will then lower payments by 5% on the next contract, or at least a good fraction of that, compared to the counterfactual.</p>
<p>Also in the long term, other effects within healthcare will dwarf this.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.apollo.com/insights-news/insights/daily-spark/is-an-agentic-bank-run-coming">Torsten Slok of Apollo worries about an ‘agentic bank run’</a> if AI agents start moving deposits around to optimize returns. Banks make a lot of money from paying very little on remarkably large amounts of deposited money.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/k8soasq8nruxzdwbdhsw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I notice that I, too, am not remotely maximizing my cash returns. I don’t get 0.1%, but I don’t get the full 5% either, and I also leave a bunch at 0% so that I don’t have to worry much about accidentally running out of money in the main checking account. If I had AI properly handling this, that I fully trusted, then we could do ‘just in time’ movement and max all that out. Right now that falls under ‘I have better things to do and don’t want to hook the AIs up to my bank accounts’ but that could change. For regular people, I expect it to be a while before they are even aware of this option, but yes on a 10-year horizon any such reliance on customer laziness should fall away.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, people retweet <a href="https://x.com/cullenroche/status/2104265339584528547">‘doomerism debunked’ statements written by AI</a> (here posted under the name Cullen Roche) that try to fool you with definitions without addressing the underlying mechanism or risk at all, a classic example of:</p>
<ol>
<li>Someone [S] warns [X] might happen.</li>
<li>People [P] respond by calling [S] a ‘doomer’ and saying [X] is misleadingly named.</li>
<li>Therefore, [P] want you to think, you don’t have to worry about [X] happening.</li>
</ol>
<p>Except, none of that means [X] won’t happen.</p>


<h4>Get Involved</h4>


<p>Lighthaven is the best event venue I know about. I will be flying there tomorrow for The Curve. Also, they are available as an event space you can rent, <a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2103883672197939571">usually as an arms length transaction</a>, and as of the last time I checked prices are highly reasonable.</p>
<p>Or, if you are wise, in this case don’t:<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2103947322346652053"> There is (or was, you won’t read this until Thursday or later) a crypto pump</a> on a ‘p(doom) coin’ going around some parts of Twitter, and one of the ways they got attention was forcibly paying Eliezer Yudkowsky some of the transaction fees, without his permission. Eliezer of course responded to tell people not to buy the coin.</p>
<p>For those who do need to be told, if you use your X Money to buy such a thing, it will quickly become your ex-money.</p>
<p>A reminder of the comments policy, since I’m getting people violating it:</p>
<ol>
<li>Humans who write things relevant to the post or previous discussion have wide berth and freedom of speech to be rather obnoxious or wrong or disagree with me and so on. Freedom of speech uber alles. To get a comment removed let alone get banned you have to try really, really hard.</li>
<li>Off-topic commercial spam and similar is still not okay, nor is flooding with the same content repeatedly, but I will try to be generous and if you are being relevant you can link to your writing or thing at least once. Basically, I’ll only act on this if I feel I have to, but don’t test me.</li>
<li>AIs are allowed to post, and you are allowed to post AI-generated content, but it should be clearly labeled in some way and in particular it needs to be interesting and not slop. If I see generic ‘AI slop’ that does not have content, and you haven’t earned some rope by providing other value, that’s a ban.</li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://claude.ai/anthropic-interviewer/your-thoughts-on-ai?from=social">You can take the Anthropic 15-minute interview about what you want from AI</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2105018785560469901">Garrison Lovely’s</a> book <a href="https://t.co/sH5soK61IA"><em>Obsolete: The AI Industry’s Trillion-Dollar Race to Replace Us</em></a> is out. As usual, if you are going to buy a book you want to see do well, do it early.</p>


<h4>Introducing</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/JensenHuang/status/2104499465055023424">The Nvidia Open Agent Safety Platform</a>, <a href="https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-open-agent-safety-platform-a-reference-for-continuous-in-silicon-agent-monitoring?ncid=so-twit-994629">bringing together OpenShell and Sentry</a>.</p>
<p>This is really the Open Agent Security Platform. A good idea, but distinct.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://github.com/NVIDIA/openshell">John Myers, Alex Watson, Ali Golshan and Ofir Arkin</a> (Nvidia Technical Blog): <a href="https://github.com/NVIDIA/openshell">NVIDIA OpenShell</a> (Apache 2.0) is an open source secure runtime for executing autonomous AI agents in sandboxed environments with kernel-level isolation. Building <a href="https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/ai/openshell/">OpenShell</a> over the past year, we have learned that every agent should run in a zero-trust environment out of the box. They need isolation, monitoring, and behavior detection. Today, we are introducing an open stack that makes this possible.</p>
<p>… OpenShell runs each agent in a sandbox and turns the operator’s instructions into a verifiable policy. Operators define which files, networks, tools, processes, and credentials an agent can access. OpenShell checks those limits before the agent runs and enforces them as it works.</p>
