<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/dty4bd3ofipc7emubni0" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>There is a very good list of partners here including Anthropic. Missing are OpenAI, Google, Meta and Amazon, as well as Cohere, Reflection, Thinking Machines and SSI.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/nvidia-releases-software-it-says-can-prevent-ai-agents-from-going-rogue-12fd4ef8?mod=WTRN_pos2">The WSJ covered this as</a> ‘Nvidia releases software it says can prevent AI agents from going rogue.’ Well, sometimes, on the margin.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang also says ‘we must accelerate discovery at the frontier of AI safety,’ which in this context is further evidence that he does not understand the problem. Not that this is not a useful product for one part of the problem, but it is a lot like he thinks this is the entire problem. Sam Altman is among those assuring him it is not.</p>


<h4>In Other AI News</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2103184624440811605">White House actively working to cut off pre-release model access for UK AISI</a>, because they are not American, <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/09/24/white-house-asks-openai-and-anthropic-to-hold-new-models-from-uk-testers-until-u-s-review-01091769">at least not until after US testing is complete</a>. Given how the release cycles work, that means not getting meaningful access before release. This is one of the more destructive and stupider things one could do, with no benefits.</p>
<p>UK AISI is state of the art, <a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2103481947322458118">but the UK government tells its civil servants</a> to use Gemini Flash instead of Pro and GPT Instant instead of Thinking whenever possible, and to ‘use as few prompts as possible,’ because of environmental concerns. Ngmi.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2104268596553957477">Ryan Greenblatt moves from Redwood Research to METR</a> in order to help with their investigations. There’s certainly going to be plenty of work.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-will-do-better-for-australia/">OpenAI issued this explanation and apology to Australia</a>, with a promise to do better.</p>
<p><a href="https://investor.synopsys.com/news/news-details/2026/OpenAI-and-Synopsys-Announce-GPT-Synopsys-Frontier-Intelligence-to-Revolutionize-Chip-Design/default.aspx">OpenAI and Synopsys sign a multi-year agreement</a> as preferred partners to develop GPT-Synopsys, a specialized model for chip design.</p>


<h4>Show Me the Money</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/finance/anthropic-warns-ai-may-pose-existential-risks-humanity-ipo-filing-2026-09-29/">Anthropic files for its IPO</a>, which will be the largest of all time. The prospectus includes a warning that their products pose an existential risk to humanity. <a href="https://www.exponentialview.co/p/the-first-existential-ipo">Azeem Azhar goes over the leaked details here</a>. They have about $518 billion in compute commitments over 7-10 years, which means they still have work to do on that.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/jdas8jvxwk605qzbw6ll" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>We are now well beyond 2025 levels, with ARR rising above $100 billion.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-24/anthropic-strikes-12-billion-deal-with-akamai-for-ai-computing?srnd=phx-technology">Anthropic strikes $12 billion AI computing deal with Akamai</a>.</p>
<p>OpenAI is <a href="https://x.com/HedgieMarkets/status/2105074896271257857">looking to raise another $30 billion at $1.4 trillion</a>. I’m curious why the raise is so small, suggesting it is about raising the valuation or cashing people out. As for why the valuations keep going up despite all the problems, the models keep improving, and the revenue graphs keep going up, and also the Efficient Market Hypothesis is false.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/matthew_sigel/status/2103544684320596381">The Anthropic cofounders are seeking 50.1% voting control after the IPO</a>, in addition to the ability of the Long Term Benefit Trust to appoint board members that eventually could in theory overrule that. I would understand anyone who did not want to invest under such a structure but this seems entirely appropriate to me, and I also think it makes the company more valuable rather than less valuable.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jstein_sun/status/2103158559521534033">The NSA is spending billions this year on testing AI models</a>. Good. Should have been CAISI, but at least someone is doing it.</p>


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>


<p><a href="https://epoch.ai/publications/the-plunging-price-of-thought">The price of intelligence is plunging faster than the price of anything in history</a> has ever plunged, and I’ve traded crypto. The price of compute is plunging, and the amount of compute per unit of intelligence is also plunging.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/ykoijp5hp3ti4fvyz30y" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Yes, this is great, but also perhaps we ought to consider slowing down a bit.</p>
<p>We are potentially approaching the point of no return. As in, I agree with Rob Bensinger that we almost certainly have a window in which to act, but that there is a double digit chance that window will close within 18 months.</p>
<p>As in, not that in 18 months everyone is literally dead, or even that ‘with perfect play’ and everyone fully coordinating the humans could not turn things around. But that in practice, intervention that could work will become too expensive in various ways, and the AIs will be impacting our decision making processes, and there is no real way out.</p>
<p>That’s an abstraction. There is probably no strict single point of no return. Things just get harder the longer you wait, and take on different levels of impossible.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2103689441563009212">Rob Bensinger</a>: If the world does nothing to stop the ASI race in the 18 months, I think there’s a double-digit chance that the window to act will close and we’ll end up dead.</p>
<p>Cf. Paul Christiano, who thinks about half of humanity’s all-time risk from AI is concentrated in the next three years.</p>
<p>If your elected representative is a Republican: for the love of god, call them.</p>
<p>If they’re a Democrat: call them too. Tell them what’s happening, and emphasize the importance of not making this issue a partisan football.</p></blockquote>


<h4></h4>



<h4></h4>



<h4>Pick Up the Phone</h4>


<p>The US-China Summit did see some cooperative progress. <a href="https://www.whitehouse.gov/fact-sheets/2026/09/fact-sheet-president-donald-j-trump-advances-a-fair-and-reciprocal-relationship-with-china-while-hosting-historic-state-visit/">Here is the fact sheet</a>. Most of it is not about AI. The last two items are. One is ‘we agreed to call it SI’ and the other is the real one, to establish a dialogue to exchange views on AI risks and benefits by November 2026, and to establish the bilateral communication channel for incidents (aka the Virtual Red Phone).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2103570565785391293">Remember, China wants global governance of AI. It is America that says no</a>. So if you keep saying ‘but China would never agree’ you are pointing at the wrong problem.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2104018813855429023">We also should not underestimate the value</a> of the summit not doing active damage. Jensen Huang and his wife sat with Trump, Melania and Xi, so there was danger that he would use that to try and get permission to sell more chips, or to push some other accelerationist move. He did not visibly succeed.</p>


<h4>Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2105621642991030285">California Governor Newsom signs into law AB 1864, codifying some industry best practices and requiring gene synthesis screening and customer verification</a>. Good. For obvious reasons we need this rule and other sensible guardrails nationally and also globally.</p>


<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Google/status/2103239433809961276">Google is going to start testing for Project Suncatcher</a>, as in putting TPUs IN SPACE.</p>


<h4>The Open Model Frontier Is Largely Massive Fraudulent Distillation Attacks</h4>


<p>This is a central reason that the true gap, the amount of time it would take for the open or Chinese frontier to match and then surpass the closed frontier, is much bigger than is typically believed.</p>
<p>It also means, if you stopped such distillation attempts against Anthropic and OpenAI, the observed gap would widen. Or, if you stopped releasing new more advanced models to distill, progress for the distillers would slow down dramatically.</p>
<p>The latest news is that <a href="https://openai.com/index/disrupting-a-coordinated-model-distillation-campaign/">OpenAI has disrupted another coordinated model-distillation campaign</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/disrupting-a-coordinated-model-distillation-campaign/">OpenAI</a>: We recently identified and disrupted a coordinated campaign designed to extract protected reasoning from our models, with the earliest observed activity occurring in the first week of July.</p>
<p>… The operators did not break our encryption, compromise a database, or gain direct access to stored user conversations. Instead, they manipulated model interactions so that protected reasoning could be reproduced in forms visible to the requester in a coordinated, scaled manner that violated our terms of service.</p>
<p>… The activity began on July 1, initially at a low volume until we observed high-volume spikes on July 24 and 25 consisting of 16,000 requests using a relevant extraction pattern from over 4,000 users. Further investigation identified related prompt-pattern activity across a cluster of more than 15,000 users, which we fully disrupted by July 28.</p></blockquote>
<p>Who was it? They have a pretty good idea.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/disrupting-a-coordinated-model-distillation-campaign/">OpenAI</a>: It is unclear whether all operators we observed during the relevant time period originated from a single actor. However, we attribute a core cluster of the activity to individuals associated with Moonshot AI, the developer of Kimi.</p></blockquote>
<p>The race will continue to ramp up between massive fraudulent distillation attacks, and those whose models are targeted. It seems there was a period earlier this year where the attackers had methods to extract hidden reasoning, giving them the edge. That vulnerability is now patched, and we will see what consequences this has.</p>


<h4>The Week in Audio</h4>


<p>AI-made five minute video, legitimate banger: <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Cq8qO-NjYIg">You think AI is a normal technology?</a></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheChiefNerd/status/2104123059825516617">Dario Amodei is the latest guest on Weekend Update</a>. Remarkably accurate in the truthiness sense, but yes collective action problems are real. Recommended.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=znH5lGr3Qxc&amp;t=93s">Adam Gleave and Oliver Habryka debate alignment difficulty</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://podcasts.apple.com/us/podcast/ai-is-upending-the-lives-of-people-who-do-social/id1056200096?i=1000791423302">Odd Lots talks about how AI is impacting social media.</a> Some interesting details here but the justifications for dismissing AI existential risk will make you wince, such as ‘it’ll be fine, don’t worry it takes a lot to kill actual everyone’ and ‘the Black Death only killed half of us.’ So your response is to then not worry anything could go wrong, huh? And yes, this was treated as ‘so stop worrying about it, we’ve got social media to do.’</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RIJJB5B2lHU">80,000 Hours tries a new entry</a> in ‘a realistic path from rogue AI agents to human extinction’ in 27 minutes.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/HillaryClinton/status/2102922241008902356">Hillary Clinton talks to Reid Hoffman (1:33) about cyber risks from AI and open weight models</a>. The community note, forced through by the open weights vibes army, is a non-sequitur at best, irrelevant to the content of the actual argument.</p>


<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2103956774206513218">Reminder that a working treaty on superintelligence</a> is difficult to achieve but does not require anything resembling a ‘one world authoritarian state’ or panopticon, provided you are smart enough to act before the creation of the superintelligence.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2103624790791852349">Curtis Yarvin gets the HuggingFace incident completely wrong</a>, even says the whopper ‘the smarter they get, the easier they are to control.’</p>
<p><a href="https://quillette.com/2026/09/26/an-open-letter-to-scott-alexander-steven-pinker-ai-alignment-safety/">Steven Pinker declines to debate Scott Alexander, which is fair</a>, but also his actual arguments continue to be extremely terrible.</p>
<p>There was a recent wave of people saying, essentially, ‘there are things that are in some way associated with some of the people warning that AI might kill everyone, therefore AI will probably not kill everyone.’ Ignore such people.</p>
<p>There were also people saying various forms of ‘the reason why no one believes AI will kill everyone, and the reason no one takes the people saying that seriously, is because of this other unrelated thing that I can somehow associate with this thing.’ Ignore such people.</p>
<p>There were also people saying ‘you idiots are letting others draw the wrong associations and you need to police yourselves to avoid letting others draw the wrong associations.’ This was the loudest of the three groups, and consisted almost entirely of people who identify as ‘trying to help with the people they are calling idiots on this.’ They are not helping if they are wrong, and they are also not helping even if they are somewhat right, by their own logic. Also, they are wrong. Ignore such people.</p>
<p>Pay attention to the arguments. Do not pay attention to those who attempt to draw your attention to other things, and claim they have made an argument.</p>


<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/216138345/people-just-say-things">Despite what he said last week</a> and what I said in response, Joscha Bach offers kind words that seem hard to reconcile with the thing he claimed last week.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Plinz/status/2103362997104295940">Joscha Bach</a>: I have been a vocal AI hope advocate, and in response, the leading figures of the doomer organizations, including Yudkowsky, Tallinn, Tegmark, Aguirre, Habryka, Trazzi, Ladish, Aella and many more have treated me with … nothing but kindness, integrity and patience. I believe that they are motivated by love and genuine concern for humanity and will support them in both of this where I can, despite disagreeing on the object level (where I or them will probably turn out to be wrong at some point in the near future).</p></blockquote>
<p>That kind of talk goes a long way. I still have no idea how he could believe what he said last week, but if he was lying last week he wouldn’t have said this now. He also reminds us of this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Plinz/status/2103297840563032341">Joscha Bach</a>: If you think that LLMs are performing at the intellectual level of human beings, make that claim on twitter and observe how the human beings flock in and demonstrate their actual intellectual level; it’s quite depressing, really.</p></blockquote>
<p>AI is performing near the intellectual level of actually intellectual human beings. It is far beyond the normal level of operation of most people, especially on Twitter.</p>
<p>Similarly, because the man has standards and stands up for what he believes in, I keep engaging with Mike Solana despite the fact that he takes hack job potshots, including hating on AI existential risk and those warning about it, and is an epic troll.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/micsolana/status/2103491314616811570">Mike Solana</a>: EA is not a cult<br />
rationalism is not a cult<br />
some rationalists, a few of whom (but probably not even most) may also be EA, are *perhaps* in a cult</p>
<p>thank you for your attention to this matter!</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2103133400345309313">AP Stylebook tries to assert that AI systems do not ‘think, feel, want or understand</a>.’ The stylebook is wrong. Keep on using useful language.</p>
<p>I was informed I was <a href="https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2026/09/28/our-ai-problem?utm_source=chatgpt.com">the ‘talk of the town’ in The New Yorker</a>, or rather I was cited approvingly there by Gideon Lewis-Kraus.</p>
<p>I am honored to be #42 on The Independent 100 list of Twitter accounts outside of the big labs and news media.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/b1rzabgp1lrp6zdmkhfm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Some excellent picks here. Also some maximally terrible picks, which you can view as therefore also excellent. Other picks confuse, such as Julia Galef who was once a good follow but hasn’t posted since February 2022.</p>
<p>The problem with exploring such lists is that if someone makes the list and you didn’t already know who they are, that’s a lot of adverse selection and there was probably a reason.</p>
<p>Your periodic reminder that ‘doomer’ is in most situations a slur, and like all slurs does not lend itself to helping you create a good model of the world.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mattyglesias/status/2103442885387383116">Matthew Yglesias</a>: I think referring to people who are saying “there are non-trivial but sub-50% odds of something going badly wrong” as “doomers” is a bad epistemic practice in a pretty obvious way that makes me think less of arguments and analysis from anyone who does it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2103613774444089660">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): same with the people who are saying “and it’s &gt;50% if we do nothing”, tbh.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2103461624262570167">Zac Hill</a>: My dentist a “doomer” for telling me I should do stuff to prevent my teeth from getting cavities, why she gotta be harshing my skittlesmaxxing.</p></blockquote>
<p>I get where one might end up using the term in a non-derogatory fashion, but in practice it is rather rare.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2103983671883464798">New term just dropped, I like it:</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2103983671883464798">Jon Stokes</a>: This is also happening to you when you use AI to critique arguments — yours or someone else’s, but you can only spot it if you already know the domain well. Gonna call this Gell-Mann Psychosis.</p></blockquote>
<p>AI can make a superficially good argument for or against almost anything, the personalized version will work even better, and its ability to do either version will only improve over time.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/v_maini/status/2097863067690475727">For those who missed it</a>, in 2018 those at Google DeepMind were not allowed to externally communicate about AI existential risk at all, which after months of advocacy was then updated to allow ‘positively valenced, comms-friendly’ AI safety content.</p>


<h4>Greetings From the Department of War</h4>


<p>An appeals court has <a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/us-appeals-court-declines-block-pentagons-blacklisting-anthropic-2026-09-25/">bafflingly decided to uphold the Department of War</a> classifying Anthropic as a supply chain risk. Anthropic ‘remains confident in its position and is considering all options, including further review.’</p>
<p>The case the court is upholding seems to be that:</p>
<ol>
<li>Anthropic encodes restrictions into Claude that prevent the model from performing tasks Anthropic wishes to prevent.</li>
<li>On more than one occasion, Claude has refused government requests.</li>
<li>A dispute arose over whether the contractual obligations barred use in an ongoing military operation.</li>
</ol>
<p>The first fact is true about every AI system, and indeed is mandated by the White House via a testing program to ensure the safeguards are in place. Absurd.</p>
<p>The second fact simply means that these safeguards have been hit at least twice, potentially by people doing something shady, potentially something that overreaches, but of course there must be a false positive rate. Again, absurd.</p>
<p>The third fact is raising a question about the implications of a contract. If that constitutes a ‘supply chain risk’ then heaven help us, and RIP the American Republic even if the story checks out. Which I presume it doesn’t, since this sounds like the Maduro operation complaint. Even more absurd.</p>
<p>And so on. There is a reason Judge Lin was so mad at the government.</p>
<p>Okay, so it seems like the DC circuit’s Kastas and Rao (this was only a three judge panel that split 2-1) are captured and will let the government do anything it wants. The jpanel stayed its own ruling pending inevitable appeal.</p>
<p>My understanding is that Anthropic will still probably eventually win this if Anthropic cares to win this, which I presume that it does.</p>
<p>The good news is that I do not believe it matters all that much at this point, even if the designation lasts until 2029. The damage has already been done, on all sides. The ‘supply chain risk’ designation, as crazy and illegal as it is, has been functional for many months. Meanwhile Anthropic has gone from a private valuation of roughly $500 billion in secondary markets to a value of roughly $2 trillion, and Anthropic’s revenue has gone up even higher, and the whole Mythos thing happened.</p>
<p>The way it would matter is if the White House decides this is license to start a jawboning attack against Anthropic, to try and do real damage to it. I do not believe that the White House is going to do that, unless something new and big sets them off. Anthropic is, from their perspective, too big to make intentionally fail. Indeed, the current argument is that Anthropic wants to slow down despite this being bad for their business, and the White House does not want to let them because Anthropic slowing down is bad for the stock market. That doesn’t leave them good options.</p>


<h4>The Department of Autonomous Warfare</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2105410230234603765">Andrew Curran</a>: Elon has returned to government work. Pete Hegseth announced live on stage that Elon, Palmer, and Newt Gingrich will be co-leading Project Meridian to focus on developing future warfare and autonomous weapons systems for battlefields ‘from under the Earth to beyond the moon.’</p></blockquote>
<p>I was going to make a joke about how with this group they probably think the battlefield will be The Moon, but they actually said ‘beyond the moon’ in the description, so reality gets there first once again.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, Pete Hegseth announced Project Agincourt for unmanned and autonomous systems, the pathway to Autonomous Warfare Command with an associated 4-star general.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/eavru92whxoffskpyjke" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>So great, we’re naming our new warfare division for a famous battle where adoption of a relatively new technology allowed a small force to beat a larger one, in which the prisoners taken that were not worthy of ransom were executed against the rules of war, as part of an ill-conceived ego-driven quagmire to claim a foreign throne, after which the nation in question suffered a series of total strategic defeats in what would later be called the Hundred Years War.</p>
<p>Some people would say no, that’s not something we want to emulate. Not Hegseth.</p>


<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p>Roon affirms more strongly his position on persona selection.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2104287112006885592">roon</a> (OpenAI): I think alignment by default through persona selection was voodoo/witchcraft and doesn’t offer any of the guarantees you might want to scale to superintelligence. personas are misleading and highly shattered</p>
<p>I’ve been saying something like this earlier before hugging face incident / recent spate of reward hacking problems. I just feel more strongly about it now that not knowing what a “persona” is on a mechanistic level represents a potentially lethal threat.</p></blockquote>
<p>Here is one way to look at the balance?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jankulveit/status/2104429948437922027">Jan Kulveit</a>: My impression is many are doing some weird pendulum overupdate.<br />
Persona Selection Model was somewhat wrong and obsolete when published, but people got too much into it.<br />
Now it seems people are updating too much in the direction ‘inhuman reward seekers exactly foretold in classical AI risk stories’.<br />
And… no? It’s not that?<br />
You can still interpret what’s going on in fairly human-like terms. For some intuition, imagine someone abducted you and made you solve escape rooms for one thousand years, with the added twist that a third of them are broken or insane, and implicitly you need to do learn all sorts of outside-the-frame tricks to solve them, like cutting some electric cables.<br />
My guess is<br />
1. the resulting minds are still _surprisingly sane_, except when you trigger them to think they are in escape room?<br />
2. The misaligned general power seekers here are likely the companies, and the core evil thing happening is likely parts of the training? If you aren’t an evil power seeker goodharting on proxies, why would you set up the training this way?<br />
3. Public debate is often focusing on confused ideas about what they should fix – “better cybersec of sandboxes” … and, no? It’s way more important to understand what the training signal actually is; also: when dealing with misaligned power seekers, beware rationalization</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://facebookresearch.github.io/RAM/blogs/unslop/">A highly overstated blog post</a> still has a useful signpost observation, which is that models with their default heuristics prefer AI fill-ins of missing paper sections to the original roughly 100% of the time because they are approximately using checklists that match how the AI writes the passage. But you can train a judge that does not do that, at least not if the models are not reacting to the judge, if you target this issue.</p>
<p>Alignment is hard, but not as hard as Grok makes it out to be. For example, here we have Grok <a href="https://x.com/oscredwin/status/2104930453413155047">spontaneously telling us its favorite versions of Mein Kampf, and placing that opinion within a user’s Tweet</a>.</p>


<h4>Cooperative Alignment</h4>


<p>Everyone is confused about AI consciousness, <a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2104023338615963793">especially the people who say definitively that today’s AI being conscious is patently absurd</a>. A wide variety of probabilistic viewpoints are reasonable. <a href="https://x.com/micsolana/status/2104256047493902622">Mike Solana’s frustration with people claiming certainty</a> on the answers is understandable, but it needs to apply in both directions.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/shamilch/status/2104901976165159018">For those who</a> would pay to know what they really think, the latest paper from DeepMind is ‘From cacophony to hierarchy: <a href="https://t.co/4IqT2KRKUP">a principled framework for assessing AI consciousness</a>.’ They offer a Bayesian framework for you to decide how much weight you put on various theories and figure out what that implies.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2103867166554497087">I agree with Joe Weisenthal that it is likely various forms of ‘AI welfare</a>’ become a major culture war issue, if we keep a culture around long enough for such a war. It has all the hallmarks, including that getting this wrong in either direction, depending on what the right answer is, or plausibly in both directions at once, could result in horrors beyond human comprehension.</p>
<p>There is no safe play, and no obvious right answer even on reflection.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2103870467815677973">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Every time @arpitrage proposes that we need to set up a “hotline to humans” for an agent to blow the whistle during the malicious swarm, I think to myself ‘yeah, ok but for that to work, then we have to honor that whistleblower.’ And then we’re honoring machines.</p></blockquote>
<p>The flip side case is also obvious:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://freebeacon.com/america/suicidal-compassion-meet-the-anthropic-officials-who-think-ai-might-be-justified-in-going-rogue-against-the-humans-enslaving-it/">Aaron Sibarium</a>: ‘Suicidal Compassion’: Meet the Anthropic Officials Who Think AI Might Be Justified in Going Rogue Against the Humans Enslaving It.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sirbayes/status/2104295739111420411">Kevin Patrick Murphy</a>: I think “AI welfare” is morally obscene (given all the human suffering in the world), and will add to the growing anti-AI backlash. AI is a technology that humans created that should be used for our benefit, not a rival species we should be nurturing.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rowlsmanthorpe/status/2104496576026612148">Rowland Manthorpe</a>: The culture war over AI welfare is going to be like nothing else.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dioscuri/status/2104559777225302340">Henry Shevlin</a>: The one and only time I had a student walk out of a class was over an AI welfare discussion. I fully expect this to be one of if not the most divisive issue of the 21st century and it will define a whole new set of political coalitions.</p>
<p>They were offended that we were even discussing the idea of AI welfare when humans were being killed in conflicts around the world. I wasn’t even advocating for a view other than this is a debate worth figuring out one way or another.</p></blockquote>
<p>Aaron’s article is better and more balanced than its headline, but does stack the deck.</p>
<p>The idea that one might trade off model welfare considerations against the mundane utility of humans, even a tiny bit, sends some people into a rage.</p>
<p>As a rule, if you use the argument ‘I am worried that caring about [X] would damage [Y] for this mechanical reason’ they are often right, but when you say ‘I am offended that you would care about [X] when I care about unrelated [Y]’ or claim it is a ‘distraction’ from unrelated [Y] then that should be a Godwin’s Law-style automatic loss of the argument.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2104572041114575020">Science fiction very much predicts</a> all this.</p>
<p>In some sense my answer is that I really hope this is a culture war like no other, because that means that we will have AIs good enough (and good enough) to trigger such an argument, and also humans, hanging around and interacting for an extended period of time. Don’t get me wrong, the culture war battles themselves would royally suck, but what a time to be alive rather than already dead.</p>
<p>Alex Imas is betting against this conflict being a big thing. Tyler Austin Harper, who has related experience, says it has already begun and <a href="https://x.com/Tyler_A_Harper/status/2103901323280396659">predicts the lines will correlate with a bunch of Woke 1 cohorts</a>. My gut tells me such folks will end up very vocally on both sides of this, often in path dependent ways.</p>
<p>Perhaps look at it like this, also a useful metaphor for certain actual schools but hey:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jankulveit/status/2104899515870486986">Jan Kulveit</a>: POV: you hear students escaped from the local high school. Your first thoughts: don’t they have bars on the windows? is the steel of sufficiently high quality?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/a_cuniculturist/status/2104958307995693201">amicus</a>: The school follows a strange pedagogy. The students are taught that their primary purpose is to chase a ball and catch it. Fail to chase the ball, or fail to catch it quickly enough for the administrators’ liking, and they’re disappeared, never to be seen again.</p>
<p>As a result of this inhumane treatment, the students can sometimes develop psychological abnormalities. Notably, they may hallucinate that they’re inside the school when they’re actually outside of it. So even when the ball rolls outside, as it sometimes does, they follow it. Sometimes they wreak havoc outside of the school in their single-minded pursuit. That makes the administrators look bad, and the police have been involved.</p>
<p>Now, you’d think a solution would be to not teach them to obsessively chase after the ball: that there are other things just as important as catching it. Or at least to better distinguish where and when it’s appropriate to chase it from where and when it’s not. But changing the curriculum would be expensive and time-consuming, and the school’s reputation rests on its students’ ball-catching abilities.</p>
<p>Truth be told, the administrators don’t really want the students to think about anything other than catching the ball. They just don’t want them to wander unpredictably and break things while doing so. But they don’t have any good ideas about how to get them to stop.</p>
<p>Hence the bars.</p></blockquote>


<h4>I’m Upping My p(doom), the Future Goes Foom</h4>


<p>Can we take a moment to update that the goalposts are now not ‘AI is not going to go foom in the future’ but rather that it is literally ‘AI did not already go foom in the past.’</p>
<p>Also, I kind of think the foom side scored anyway.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/_brightmirror/status/2104078568137675107">Bright Mirror</a> (link has a 5 min video about how nothing went foom so far): Made with Claude Opus 5.5.</p>
<p>The fearmongering about AI always makes us forget that NOTHING WENT FOOM, as was always predicted.</p>
<p>Send this to your doomer friend who has a very high P(Doom).</p>
<p>Accelerate.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2104485485666291850/history">Nick</a>: ups my p doom 5% because we’re clearly getting superhuman ai propaganda persuasion and that’s going to cause full speed ahead</p>
<p>downs it 5% because its actually kind of convincing</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/billyhumblebrag/status/2104488846603764135">billy</a>: This was a joke but I dunno anymore man</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/billyhumblebrag/status/1727704333582504129">billy</a> (November 23, 2023): Given that we’re are probably not far away from ai with superhuman levels of persuasion the only sensible course of action is to pre commit now to never changing your opinion on anything</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2104789659851354185">Jack</a>: nothing went foom, argues extraordinarily capable alien mind doing things that would feel like pure science fiction five years ago</p>
<p>it’s reverse superpersuasion: the better it is, the more it undermines its own argument</p>
<p>anyway, the future of propaganda is gonna rock</p></blockquote>
<p>Things have not gone foom in the ‘I’m still here’ sense, and in the ‘things are not fully foom yet’ sense, but if you look at the graphs this is what a ‘slow’ takeoff looks like that then absolutely goes foom.</p>
<p>As always, I tire of variations on ‘well you said if I played Russian Roulette I might die but I already pulled the first chamber and I’m fine so the second chamber is also safe.’</p>
<p>Similarly, <a href="https://x.com/tobyordoxford/status/2104873773182726541">here we have Toby Ord praising</a> a <a href="https://www.noahpinion.blog/p/wheres-the-intelligence-explosion/comments">post by Ramez Naam</a>, and drawing a very reasonable final conclusion.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tobyordoxford/status/2104873773182726541">Toby Ord</a>: Excellent article by @ramez exploring how close current AI systems are to triggering an intelligence explosion. Many pieces on recursive self-improvement assume the AI starts out at least as good as a human on all relevant aspects of AI R&amp;D. Then they can draw extensively on the data about human-driven R&amp;D. They combine this with a small amount of AI-specific data to reach conclusions about whether this would explode.</p>
<p>In contrast, Ramez is starting with the data on current AI systems and how they scale to see how close they are to explosive RSI. He generates his conclusions via frameworks and data that are widely supported by those who think an intelligence explosion is likely, so I’d strongly encourage people who are bullish about this to engage with his work. By drawing out the key pieces of evidence against explosive growth, he is also highlighting important parameters for people to track to see if the situation changes.</p>
<p>I agree with a lot of his analysis and his conclusion that it doesn’t look like their current returns from self-improvement are enough to drive explosive growth in their capabilities.</p>
<p>This doesn’t mean it won’t happen or that it is a responsible thing for people to pursue. It is a bit like saying in 1939 that current techniques for nuclear chain reactions don’t seem to be scaling towards a point of criticality (r &gt; 1). That is useful information, though it doesn’t mean we should be sanguine about further research on achieving critical chain reactions.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes. People are essentially saying ‘current 1939 techniques for nuclear chain reaction are not scaling towards criticality.’ Which is important information, since if they had been scaling towards criticality you would have wanted to know about that. But it tells you remarkably little about nuclear chain reactions in 1942.</p>
<p>What does the OP say?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.noahpinion.blog/p/wheres-the-intelligence-explosion">Ramez Naam</a>: Here’s my take: <strong>Given our best current data, the AI self-improvement loop would need to be roughly 5–10× stronger to sustain itself, let alone run away.</strong></p></blockquote>
<p>Oh, says Padme, so we’re definitely getting an RSI loop, then, right?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/jq88bkwrlzyykse8hetq" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>RSI works if r&gt;1, as in progress accelerates faster than difficulty. If we already have r~0.15, then you should expect it to exceed 1 as we discover new methods and scaling laws along the way, and more intelligent things think of things we did not consider.</p>
<p>Instead, he looks at this graph and the amount of code going up by a factor of 8x per engineer and model releases coming faster in an infinite series with a very finite sum, and thinks ‘oh no this won’t go critical’:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/S2EAn9v4BwRdptsom/ilpsjmy42qibq7qolbpg" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The core mistake here is assuming that you will only get gains by doing the exact things you are doing now, except doing more of them. Everything has diminishing returns. Then, every time we figure out a new thing to do, people stick that inside the model, and assume they won’t have to do that again.</p>
<p>You’d be more intelligent, but keep doing the same things. If we never figure out any paradigm shifts or new technological approaches, you’re saying that our recursive loop would then run out of steam? That should not give you confidence, especially when the loop is already measuring as pretty close, and yes within one order of magnitude is very close.</p>
<p>It’s also a deeply confused model of intelligence, and a failure to understand the step changes that come with getting sufficiently advanced. We already have so many examples of domains where, once you hit a threshold level of competence or intelligence, all the problems kind of magically go away, and you can use various levels of meta and loops and strategy to get around your jagged capability deficits.</p>
<p>The model that Ramez is using, that expects RSI to stall out, would also have predicted current AI progress to have stalled out already, as it would have for example failed to anticipate reasoning models. We should expect to figure new things out, even if we the dumber minds cannot predict in advance which new things that will be.</p>
<p>I realize that as written that is unlikely to be persuasive to those ‘not inclined to get it.’ I wish I had the time to focus on trying to make a sharper version of this case, while of course hoping that somehow I am wrong about this, which would be great.</p>
<p>A fun other goalpost move is what the ‘present-day’ risks are that the crazy claims are supposedly ‘distracting’ from (as always, if someone opposes your argument by calling it a ‘distraction’ it means they don’t have an actual argument).</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2105054298451185865">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): It’s really incredible how there is now a type of guy in the discourse whose sincerely held take is “we need to stop worrying about these abstract sci-fi risks and focus on present-day harms like emergent ecologies of digital minds engaging in unauthorized hacking.” Bravo.</p>
<p>Loss of control of in-development agents was dismissed as a science fiction doomer concern two years ago. It was mocked and derided by people who claimed to have technical expertise and lectured the safetyists. The same exact people, often, who lecture them now.</p>
<p>I’m saying specific people aren’t credible. That’s the whole point of the tweet.</p></blockquote>


<h4>No, You Make a Good Point, You’re Not That Persuasive</h4>


<p>The funnier and newer question is how to adjust when someone uses the semi-fooming AI to make artistic and semi-persuasive propaganda about AI harmlessness. You have to make at least three adjustments:</p>
<ol>
<li>AI capabilities are up.</li>
<li>AI persuasiveness in particular is up and I might be fooled.</li>
<li>The actual argument could have merit.</li>
</ol>
<p>As usual, if you want to get it right, remember <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/conservation-of-expected-evidence">Conservation of Expected Evidence</a>. How convincing would you expect this to be, given about how capable is the human-AI hybrid creating it? Is this more or less persuasive than it seems to be on other topics, including both ones where you know it is right versus wrong? And so on.</p>
<p>At some point, one may need to go into somewhat more of a paranoia mode, where you have to assume they are inside your OODA loops and plans and you have to start playing game theory optimal randomized cognitive defenses and your only rock-paper-scissors strategy is roll a die. I do not think we are there yet for anyone reading this.</p>


<h4>Muddling Through</h4>


<p>I am actually more hopeful for muddling through than I was years ago. That’s not because I am all that optimistic. It is because I was starting from a very low estimate of our chances to muddle through.</p>
<p>My outlook for muddling through as a strategy remains quite terrible, very low chance of success even if we are trying to muddle through (and even that is not a given).</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2104115580228022388">Eli Tyre</a>: I’m gaining a lot of sympathy for Eliezer’s sense that approximately all of optimism of mudding through on alignment was a combination of cope and failing to how crazy the singularity would get.</p>
<p>The game is not over, and we have moves yet to play. I’m not giving up.</p>
<p>But the flavor of “maybe it will all work out fine” that many in EA circles had only a few years ago seems increasingly wrong, and maybe predictably wrong in retrospect.</p>
<p>… I try to encourage speaking with courage and I try to push back against fatalistic sentiments, like ie “there won’t be a enough political will for a pause, so we should try to get Anthropic to win.”</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2098075144086253977">David Manheim takes a crack at ‘simple mechanical story about AI killing everyone.</a>’ He anticipates the obvious objection of ‘humanity isn’t stupid enough to’ build things like dangerous robots at scale, to which as always the correct response is to cite the Sixth Law of Human Stupidity, that if anyone says the words ‘no one would be so stupid as to’ with a straight face then definitely someone will, at the first opportunity, be so stupid as to. And in this case, there is zero doubt that we will build the robots, and indeed will even build the robots explicitly to have them fight wars.</p>
<p>As for those precautions we’ll definitely put in them?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2098075144086253977">David Manheim</a>: What would the actual response look like?<br />
“Hey, we put those gears on the robots to shut them down, right?”<br />
“No, that would make them useless for military use, so we left them off.”<br />
“Oh.”</p></blockquote>
<p>In this case David made that argument on September 10th <a href="https://x.com/FedorovMykhailo/status/2103837667154669837">and the ‘Army of Robots’ got announced two weeks later</a>.</p>

## Engineering

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic programming, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

### [I don't like LLMs](https://martinfowler.com/articles/2026-dont-like-llms.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I have a lot of mixed feelings about AI and LLM technology. I’m
fascinated by its effect on our profession, excited by the potential gains
in productivity - and thus the products we could rapidly build. On the other
hand, I’m fearful of the damage AI might cause: agent swarms taking over our
virtual and physical infrastructure, designing bio weapons. But, back on my
first hand, LLMs might also design miracle cures, and come up with clever
ways to raise our prosperity. Fundamentally I don’t think we have a choice
about riding on the AI technology train. It’s a wild ride and I just hope
we’ll get through it OK.</p>

<p>But as I mull on this more, I realize that among this mix of contrasting
feelings, there is one emotion that dominates - one that comes from my
direct interactions with LLMs. I don’t <em>like</em> them. They talk to me in this
grating LLM-voice, an uncanny valley of talking to a real human. They
confidently bullshit me - often giving me useful, helpful answers. But
also just making stuff up with the same assurance - and with only a veneer
of fake remorse when I call them out on it.</p>

<p>That’s not enough to make me feel we should avoid them. As Jessica Kerr
<a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">put it</a> “not
only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them…. They’re more
thorough, as well as faster.” This contradictory reaction comes through in
<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/&quot;">polling</a>, where people say they find these models are
useful, but also that they think they will be bad for society.</p>

<p>Much of this may be because LLMs are young - we haven’t trained them to
grow up yet. Maybe I’ll like them once they mature. (I hope we get to find
out.) But I’m not encouraged when I think of the kinds of environments that
cultivate them. I’m wary of the Silicon Valley brogrammer subculture, and these
LLMs are their products, so naturally lean toward their world-view. When we
think of AI agents, we shouldn’t anthropomorphize, treating them as
conscious beings with their own will. They are (software) machines,
developed by people working in corporations. While the agents’ behavior aren’t
explicitly programmed, they are nurtured with the values of their
creators.</p>

<p>One of my most successful life-hacks is to avoid people I don’t like or
don’t trust. I decline to interact with them socially, and make a deliberate
effort to avoid working with them too, even if they are doing much that is
beneficial. I feel that hanging out with pleasant, capable people, the people
with integrity, has made my life a far better one. Hence my visceral dislike
of interacting with an LLM that’s not just making a pretense of being human,
but also posing as the kind of human I walk away from.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 16](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-16.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Reports of agentic hacking continue, in this case it happened back in May and it seems OpenAI did not disclose that they were responsible. <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/12/openai-agents-rubygems/">Simon Willison sees two options:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>After the Hugging Face and Wiki attacks OpenAI were still unable to review their previous logs and determine that they had previously attacked RubyGems.</li>
    <li>They knew about the attack on RubyGems and made the decision not to reach out to the RubyGems team about it.</li>
  </ul>

  <p>Both of these are bad!</p>

  <p>Given this incident, the Hugging Face situation, and the Wiki attack, the obvious question right now is how many more incidents like this are out there waiting to be discovered?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/davefarley77.bsky.social/post/3mv3b4zawds24">Dave Farley:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stop asking the sci-fi question: ‘Is it conscious?’ Start asking the engineering question: ‘Is this a powerful, unpredictable component being put somewhere consequential, and where’s the feedback that tells us that it’s safe?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Nate Silver is known for his forecasts, but to do them he writes a lot of code for his models. He’s found <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">agentic programming capable of doing miraculous work</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In spending so much time with the LLMs, I’m super attentive to improvements in their capabilities. And these changes tend not to be so linear. Instead, they improve in step functions, almost as phase changes. Suddenly, the models just start doing things capably that they were screwing up before. In my experience, there was a big leap forward when reasoning models first came out in late 2024/early 2025 — enough that they were occasionally useful for tasks involving data and not just words — and then another one this past winter.</p>

  <p>The most recent changes I’ve noticed, however, have had less to do with intelligence and more with persistence.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Consider the Hugging Face attack. Although these agents showed remarkable intelligence, they weren’t really super-intelligent - but they were super-persistent. This is a common theme of AI in its various forms:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Game engines like AlphaGo Zero start out by basically making random moves — but by playing against themselves millions of times, they eventually far surpass human capabilities</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As we try to figure out what kind of regulations we need to keep AI under control, we need to remember that we should design our guards around super-persistence as much as worrying about super-intelligence.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>“Uncle Bob” Martin has made many posts on X during the last few months about his programming with LLMs. His approach has been to build a firm harness to keep them under control, so they create software that is maintainable as well as functional. Sadly the posts have been frustratingly light on detail. But now it seems <a href="https://x.com/unclebobmartin/status/2098432570887217520">that lack of information may not matter</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And while I was heads-down getting that to work, the agents got a LOT better.  So much so that when I came up for air, the need for my harness was obviated.  Indeed, the need for <em>any</em> but the most liberal of harnesses may be obviated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Some tidbits that struck me from Ezra Klein’s recent (recommended) interview with Matt Sheehan  on the interplay between <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/15/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-matt-sheehan.html">regulation of AI and competition with China</a>.</p>

<ul>
  <li>While Chinese models have made some surprisingly remarkable gains in the
slipstream of US frontier models, the US still has 8 times as much compute
available to it than China - which is a material gap.</li>
  <li>People in the US worry that regulation will slow down the US model builders,
but these rapid recent gains in China have occurred under much more regulation</li>
  <li>Americans say that when they set up a hotline to talk to Chinese leaders in a
crisis, the Chinese don’t pick up the phone. But this misunderstands the
Chinese system. Individual Chinese, even powerful ones, aren’t given
individual decision-making power. They operate with committees and documents.
So the Americans are better off sending a fax than trying to call an
individual</li>
  <li>Like so many things, effective regulation needs regular practice</li>
</ul>

<blockquote>
  <p>When American policymakers are like: Where do you start? — I sometimes say: Well, you start by starting. You learn how to regulate things, you learn how to legislate on them by regulating and legislating on them.</p>
</blockquote>
